# China Handbook 1950 — Chronologies

1,492 entries, LLM-transcribed. These entries were transcribed by the vision model of Opus 5.5. Before using any of these details, you must verify specific details with the original source which is linked to whenever possible.


## Chronology of Major Events — Chronology of Major Events

**1911 Oct. 10** Revolutionaries staged uprising at Wuchang; founding of the Chinese Republic. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1912 Jan. 1** Dr. Sun Yat-sen became first Provisional President of the Republic of China. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1912 Jan. 26** Hsuan Tung, last emperor of Ching dynasty, abdicated. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1912 Mar. 10** Yuan Shih-kai became Provisional President of China, succeeding Dr. Sun Yat-sen, who had resigned. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1912 Mar. 11** A provisional constitution, containing 56 articles, promulgated. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1913 July 12** Second Revolution under leadership of Kuomintang leader Huang Hsing opposing Yuan Shih-kai's dictatorial rule. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1913 Sept. 1** Second Revolution failed. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1913 Oct. 10** Yuan Shih-kai formally assumed Presidency. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1914 Aug. 6** China declared neutrality in World War I. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1914 Sept.** Japanese troops seized German concessions in Shantung, occupying Tsingtao-Tsinan railway. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1915 Jan. 18** Japan presented 21 demands to Peking Government. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1915 May 9** Yuan Shih-kai accepted in part the 21 demands. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1915 Dec. 23** Yuan Shih-kai ascended the imperial throne. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1915 Dec. 25** Tsai Ngo and Tang Chi-yao started revolt against Yuan Shih-kai in Yunnan Province. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1916 June 5** Yuan Shih-kai died. Republican form of government was restored. Li Yuan-hung became president. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1917 July 12** Restoration movement of Manchu dynasty led by Chang Hsun failed. President Li Yuan-hung, who had sought refuge in the Legation Quarter during the coup, refused to resume office, and was succeeded by Feng Kuo-chang. Tuan Chi-jui and his Anfu clique came into power. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1917 Aug. 14** China declared war on Germany and Austria-Hungary. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1917 Aug. 25** Dr. Sun Yat-sen formed military government in Canton. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1918 June 5** Dr. Sun Yat-sen and other Kuomintang leaders ousted from Canton by Kwangsi faction led by Chen Chun-hsuan. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1918 July 12** A new parliament, "Tuchuns' Parliament," met in Peking. Two months later, it elected Hsu Shih-chang president. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1919 May 4** Students demonstrated in Peking, leading to dismissal of Tsao Ju-lin, Chang Tsung-hsiang and Lu Tsung-yu, officials responsible for Government's pro-Japanese policy. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1919 July** China refused to ratify Versailles Treaty because of Shantung problem. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1920 July 12** Chang Tso-lin and Tsao Kun joined forces and brought about downfall of Tuan Chi-jui and Anfu clique. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1920 Sept. 29** Peking Government broke diplomatic relations with Czarist Russia. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1921 May 5** Dr. Sun Yat-sen assumed presidency of newly-formed government in Kwangtung. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1921 Nov. 11** The Washington Conference convened under U. S. auspices to effect armament limitation and settle outstanding Pacific and Far Eastern questions. — p. 98 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1922 Feb. 4** The Shantung problem settled at the Washington Conference. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1922 Feb. 6** The Nine-Power Treaty concluded in Washington, with contracting powers agreeing to respect sovereignty, independence, and territorial and administrative integrity of China. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1922 Apr. 28** Civil war between Wu Pei-fu and Chang Tso-lin started. Following defeat of Chang by Wu's forces, President Hsu Shih-chang resigned. After delay, Li Yuan-hung, residing in Tientsin since his 1917 resignation, resumed Presidency. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1922 June 16** Chen Chun-ming revolted against Dr. Sun Yat-sen in Canton. Dr. Sun fled to Shanghai. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1922 Dec. 26** Dr. Sun Yat-sen issued manifesto urging unification of China through peaceful means. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1923 Feb. 15** Dr. Sun Yat-sen returned to Canton following defeat of Chen Chun-ming. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1923 Oct. 5** Tsao Kun elected president of China. A new constitution promulgated on October 10. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1924 Jan. 20** First National Congress of Kuomintang in Canton adopted policy of cooperation with Soviet Russia and Chinese Communist party. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1924 May 31** China established diplomatic relations with Soviet Union. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1924 June 16** Whampoa Military Academy founded with Chiang Kai-shek as principal. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1924 Oct.** The second Fengtien-Chihli war, resulting in defeat of General Wu Pei-fu and resignation of Tsao Kun. Tuan Chi-jui became provisional chief executive. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1924 Dec. 4** Dr. Sun Yat-sen arrived in Tientsin at invitation of Tuan Chi-jui. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1925 Mar. 12** Dr. Sun Yat-sen died in Peking (now Peiping) at age of 59. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1925 Oct. 26** Special conference for revision of Chinese tariff in Peking, 13 powers participating, halted by China's internal disturbances. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1925 Dec. 23** Kuomintang right wing at Western Hills conference outside Peking moved to dismiss Borodin, Soviet political adviser, and Gen. Galen, Soviet military adviser to revolutionary government in Canton. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1926 Jan.** The Kuomintang's Second National Congress decided to retain Soviet advisors, censured right wing. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1926 Apr.** Feng Yu-hsieng's Kuominchun defeated by joint forces of Chang Tso-lin and Wu Pei-fu. Tuan Chi-jui resigned as provisional chief executive. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1926 June 6** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek became commander-in-chief of National Revolutionary Forces and launched Northern Expedition. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1926 July-Nov.** The Northern Expeditionary Forces gained series of victories, taking Changsa in July, Hankow in October, Kiukiang and Nanchang in November. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 Jan. 4** The Northern Expeditionary Forces took British concessions in Hankow and Kiukiang by force. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 Feb.** Kuomintang left wing set up revolutionary government in Wuhan. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 Mar. 21** The Northern Expeditionary Forces occupied Shanghai and Nanking. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 Apr. 18** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek set up National Government in Nanking. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 June 1** The Wuhan Government decided to dismiss Borodin and other Soviet advisers. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 Aug. 1** Chinese Communists staged uprising in Nanchang in revolt against National Government. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 Sept. 6** Merger of Wuhan and Nanking Governments completed. Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek resigned in interest of party unity. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1927 Dec. 15** The National Government severed relations with Soviet Russia. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 Jan. 1** The National Government ordered Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek to resume post of commander-in-chief of National Revolutionary Forces, and to continue the Northern Expedition to unify the country. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 May 1** The National Revolutionary Forces occupied Tsinan. Chang Tso-lin ordered general retreat of his troops into the northeast. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 June 4** Chang Tso-lin killed when his train was blown up by Japanese agents near Mukden. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 June 9** The Northern Expeditionary Forces occupied Peking, renamed Peiping. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 July 7** The National Government announced plan to abrogate unequal treaties and agreements contracted by defunct regimes. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 Nov. 1** The Central Bank of China established. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 Nov.-Dec.** The United States, Britain and France formally recognized the National Government as the de jure Government of the Republic of China. These powers also recognized China's tariff autonomy. — p. 99 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1928 Dec. 29** Chang Hsueh-liang of the northeast pledged his allegiance to the National Government, thus bringing about the unification of the entire country. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1929 Jan. 1** Military Reorganization and Disbandment Conference opened in Nanking under the chairmanship of Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek. A month later adopted important resolutions to reduce Chinese troops. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1929 Mar. 15** The Kuomintang's Third National Congress convened in Nanking. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1929 May 24** The Government decided to launch punitive campaign against Feng Yu-hsiang, who revolted in Honan. Feng defeated after defection of his principal subordinates. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1929 June 6** The state burial of Dr. Sun Yat-sen. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1929 June 12** The Kuomintang decided to end period of political tutelage by 1935. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1929 July 9** Chang Hsueh-liang planned to recover Chinese Eastern railway from Soviet control. Failed owing to armed Soviet opposition. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1929 Dec. 5** Tang Sheng-chih revolted in Honan against National Government. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 Jan. 6** Tang Sheng-chih retired and handed his command to subordinates. One week later remnants of Tang's troops were disarmed. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 Apr. 1** Yen Hsi-shan and Feng Yu-hsiang revolted in north China. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 Apr. 18** Britain returned Weihaiwei to China. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 June** Severe fighting took place between rebel troops and government forces along the Peiping-Hankow, Tientsin-Pukow, Lunghai railways. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 July 13** Rebels held an Enlarged Plenary Session of Kuomintang in Peiping to set up separate government under Wang Ching-wei. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 Sept. 14** Following his defeat in Shantung, Yen retired from public life in Shihchiachuang. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 Sept. 18** Chang Hsueh-liang intervened for government by sending troops to Peiping. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 Oct. 10** With the retirement of the northern coalition leaders, dissolution of the Enlarged Conference, triumphal return of Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek from the war fronts to Nanking, and acceptance by Chang Hsueh-liang of government appointment, the protracted campaign ended in the reunification of the country. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1930 Nov. 12** The Kuomintang Fourth National Congress five-day conference closed November 18. Organic law of the National Government revised, the Central Government and National Army reorganized and the National People's Convention to be convoked on May 5, 1931. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Jan. 1** National Government announced abolition of likin dues and other duties hampering domestic trade. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Mar. 2** Hu Han-min resigned presidency of the Legislative Yuan due to difference of opinion with Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek on the Provisional Constitution. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 May 5** The National People's Convention held in Nanking under the chairmanship of Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 May 12** The Provisional Constitution adopted. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 May 27** A dissension movement started in Canton. Wang Ching-wei, Tang Shao-yi, Chen Chi-tang and other Kuomintang leaders met in an extraordinary session of the Kuomintang Central Executive Committee to plot overthrow of the National Government. The dissidents issued a circular telegram urging Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek to retire. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 June 1** The Provisional Constitution was promulgated. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 June 11** Tibetan troops invaded Sikang. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 June 21** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek left for Nanchang to assume personal command of the Communist-suppression campaign. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 July 4** Korean immigrants occupied Wanpaoshan in Kirin at instigation of Japanese militarists. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 July-Sept.** Sixteen provinces in north and central China reported disastrous floods. Hankow, Wuchang and other central China cities inundated. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Sept. 13** Canton forces invaded Hunan, driving toward Hengyang. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Sept. 18** Japanese troops occupied Mukden in surprise attack. By end of month, important cities in Liaoning and Kirin fell into Japanese hands in rapid succession. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Sept. 24** Japanese planes dropped bombs on Chinchow and other cities along Peiping-Liaoning railway. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Oct. 27** Nanking and Canton representatives met in Shanghai for peace negotiations. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Nov. 10** Japanese agents instigated uprising in Tientsin. In ensuing confusion, smuggled Henry Pu Yi to the Northeast. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Nov. 21** Gen. Ma Chan-shan offered resistance to Japanese invaders in Heilungkiang. — p. 100 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Dec. 15** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek retired for second time in interest of party unity. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1931 Dec. 28** National Government was reorganized. Lin Sen made Chairman of the National Government, and Dr. Sun Fo, president of the Executive Yuan. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Jan. 1** The new government was inaugurated. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Jan. 2** Japanese entered Chinchow. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Jan. 28** Kuomintang Central Executive Committee accepted resignation of Dr. Sun Fo as president of Executive Yuan, and appointed Wang Ching-wei to fill vacancy. Dr. Sun named president of the Legislative Yuan. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932** Fighting started in Shanghai between Japanese troops and 19th Route Army. National Military Council created with Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek as chairman. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Jan. 30** The National Government moved to Loyang in face of threat of Japanese attack on Nanking. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Feb. 7** National Government and newly-created National Military Council issued circular telegram calling rank and file of National forces to arms. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Mar. 1** Kuomintang CEC, held second plenary session in Loyang to devise measures to cope with national crisis. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Mar. 9** Henry Pu Yi sworn in as Chief Executive of Japanese puppet state "Manchukuo" in northeast with Changchun as capital. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Mar. 14** League of Nations' Lytton Commission of Enquiry arrived in Shanghai; reached Nanking March 26, Peiping April 9, then proceeded to Manchuria, where it remained for about six weeks. After second visit to Peiping and Tokyo, commission stayed in Peiping on July 20 to draft report. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 April 7** The National Emergency Conference in Loyang decided on prolonged resistance against Japanese aggression. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 April 28** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek arrived in Hankow to campaign against communists who took advantage of Manchurian situation to resume activities in central China. Communists in Hupeh, Honan and Anhwei completely routed. Communist Hupeh border strongholds at Chinchiatsai and Hsinchi captured. By end of year communists were dislodged from Central China. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 May 1** China and Japan agreed on armistice, bringing end to military conflict in Shanghai. The 19th Route Army transferred from Shanghai to Fukien. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Aug. 15** National Government accepted resignation of Chang Hsueh-liang, Pacification Commissioner at Peiping, and ordered formation of Peiping branch military council. Chang's retirement was persistently sought by Wang Ching-wei. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Dec. 1** National Government moved back to Nanking from Loyang. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Dec. 12** The government announced decision to resume diplomatic relations with Soviet Russia. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1932 Dec. 16** Third plenary session of Kuomintang Fourth CEC opened in Nanking. Proposal adopted to convoke National Assembly in March, 1935, to adopt constitution for the country. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Jan. 1** Japanese troops outside Great Wall attacked Shanhaikwan, took city Jan. 3. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Feb. 27** Japanese started general offensive in Jehol, captured Chengteh March 3. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Mar. 6** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek arrived in Paoting to preside over military conference; took command of troops under Chang Hsueh-liang. General Ho Ying-chin made commander-in-chief of Chinese troops in north China next day. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Mar. 30** Kuomintang decided to call emergency meeting of Central Executive Committee on July 1, later opposed by Canton leaders including Hu Han-min and Chen Chi-tang. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 April** Fighting in north China widened. Several strategic passes along Great Wall, notably Kupeikow and Hsifengkow, fell to Japanese. Japanese continued advance to Luanchow ready to attack Peiping and Tientsin. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 May 27** Feng Yu-hsiang, using national crisis, revolted in Kalgan. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 May 31** The Sino-Japanese Tangku Armistice agreement signed, ending hostilities in north China. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 June 1** The government, to appease southwest leaders, postponed emergency meeting of Kuomintang CEC. Announced instead Fifth National Congress for November 12. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Aug. 6** Feng announced his retirement after revolt failed. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Nov. 20** Leaders of 19th Route Army announced "people's government" in Fukien and declared independence. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Nov. 23** Government started moving troops against Fukien. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Nov. 26** Government sent peace mission to Canton and Hongkong to persuade Hu Han-min and other southern CEC members to attend the fourth plenary session of CEC, scheduled for December 20. — p. 101 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1933 Dec.** Severe fighting began between Government and Fukien rebels. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Jan. 15** Fukien rebellion collapsed; 19th Route Army reorganized. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Jan. 20** Kuomintang CEC fourth plenary session, postponed for one month, formally opened in Nanking. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Jan. 25** Lin Sen reelected chairman of National Government for a second term by plenary session. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Jan. 31** Generalissimo Chiang and Wang Ching-wei issued joint statement reaffirming government's policy to suppress the communist rebellion. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934** Chang Hsueh-liang returned from Italy and appointed in February deputy-commander of bandit suppression army in Hupeh, Honan and Anhwei. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Mar. 1** Henry Pu Yi enthroned as "Emperor of Manchukuo" in Changchun. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Mar. 11** New Life Movement launched in Nanchang. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 July 8-13** Important communist bases in south Kiangsi captured by government troops. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Oct. 9** Government sent Wang Ching-wei and later Wang Chung-hui to Hongkong to effect reconciliation between Canton faction and Central Government. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Oct. 16** Legislative Yuan approved revised Draft Constitution. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Oct. 24** Generalissimo and Madame Chiang Kai-shek arrived in Peiping for an extensive tour of China's northwest. Itinerary covered Lanchow, Ningsia, Kalgan, Kweisui and Taiyuan. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Nov. 10** Government troops captured Changting in Fukien from communists. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Nov. 11** Government troops captured Juichin, capital of communist government in Kiangsi. Main force of rebels broken, remnants fled across Hunan, Szechwan and Kweichow. Retreat known as "long march" to the northwest. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1934 Dec. 17** The Southwest Political Council, meeting in Canton, resolved to support Central Government. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Jan. 29** Japanese troops invaded eastern Chahar. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Mar. 11** China protested to Moscow against transfer of Chinese Eastern railway to "Manchukuo." — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Mar. 27** Generalissimo Chiang arrived in Kweiyang and established headquarters there to supervise campaign against communist remnants fleeing across southwestern provinces. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 June 5** Japanese troops, holding maneuvers and military conferences in Tientsin, demanded removal of northeastern troops from Hopei and Chahar, and closing down of Kuomintang organs in both provinces. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 July 1** Government accepted Japanese demands under duress. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Sept. 23** British mission headed by Sir Frederick Leith-Ross, chief economic adviser to the British Government, arrived in Nanking, and stayed in China for about nine months. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Nov. 2** Government appointed Generalissimo Chiang concurrently commander-in-chief of bandit suppression forces in northwest, and Chang Hsueh-liang, deputy commander-in-chief. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Nov. 3** National Government issued proclamation, nationalizing all silver coins and making the notes issued by the Central Bank of China, Bank of China and Bank of Communications legal tender. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Nov. 11** Japanese troops created reign of terror in Peiping and Tientsin by demanding arrest of Chinese educators, officials and students. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Nov. 12** Fifth National Congress of Kuomintang, postponed twice as concession to Southwest party leaders, formally convened, with Canton representatives participating. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1935 Nov. 19** Generalissimo Chiang explained China's foreign policy as striving for peace while there is hope of peace, fighting only when driven to last extremity. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Mar. 29** Soviet Russia announced conclusion of mutual assistance pact with Outer Mongolia. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 May 5** Government promulgated the Draft Constitution. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 May 12** Hu Han-min, veteran Kuomintang leader and chairman of the CEC standing committee, died in Canton. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 June 9** Kwangsi military leaders called upon government to mobilize nation's military forces against Japan. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 June 22** Chen Chi-tang set up separate military council in Canton and sent troops to invade Hunan. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 June 28** Prince Teh of Inner Mongolia declared independence at Pailinmiao. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 July 1** Kwangtung's air force deserted Chen Chi-tang and pledged allegiance to Central Government. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 July 9** Gen. Yu Han-mou, one of Chen Chi-tang's commanders, deserted Chen and flew to Nanking. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 July 13** Second plenary session of Kuomintang CEC, meeting in Nanking, resolved to abolish Southwest Political Council and appointed Yu Han-mou commander-in-chief of Kwangtung forces. — p. 102 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Aug. 19** Kwangsi set up independent regime in Kweilin. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Sept. 16** Kwangsi problem amicably settled. Gen. Li Tsung-jen assumed office as director of Kwangsi Pacification Headquarters. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Oct. 15** Standing Committee of Kuomintang CEC decided to postpone convocation of National Assembly, originally scheduled for November 12, 1936. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Oct. 17** Japanese nationals started evacuation from cities along Yangtze river. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Oct. 18** Puppet troops in Inner Mongolia invaded eastern Suiyuan. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Oct. 31** Nation-wide celebration of Generalissimo Chiang's birthday. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Nov. 3-4** Japanese troops in north China held two-day maneuver in Peiping-Tientsin area. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Nov. 16** Severe fighting developed in eastern Suiyuan, with Gen. Fu Tso-yi's troops offering stiff resistance to Inner Mongolian invasion. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Nov. 24** Gen. Fu's forces captured Pailinmiao in counter-attack, inflicting some 1,000 casualties on puppet troops. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Dec. 12** Chang Hsueh-liang mutinied in Sian, holding Generalissimo Chiang and other high-ranking Government officials in confinement. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Dec. 22** Madame Chiang, accompanied by W. H. Donald and T. V. Soong, flew to Sian. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1936 Dec. 25** Generalissimo and Madame Chiang returned safely to Loyang. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Jan. 4** Chang Hsueh-liang was sentenced to ten years imprisonment by special military tribunal. The National Government, at request of Generalissimo Chiang, commuted his sentence and remanded him to National Military Council for discipline. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Feb. 10** Government troops took over garrison duties in Sian. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Feb. 15** Third plenary session of Kuomintang Fifth CEC convened in Nanking until Feb. 22. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Feb. 19** Kuomintang CEC rejected Eight-Point Program advocated by Chang Hsueh-liang and others in Sian coup; reaffirmed the government policy to eradicate communism in China. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Feb. 20** CEC resolved to convene National Assembly November 12 to enact national constitution. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 April-May** Japanese troops in north China rushed reinforcements to Peiping-Tientsin area and held large-scale maneuvers around the two northern cities. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 May 27** Generalissimo Chiang, at Kuling, summoned national leaders for conference. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 7** Japanese soldiers in night maneuvers in vicinity of Lukouchiao (Marco Polo Bridge), west of Peiping, attacked Wanping city. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 10** Ministry of Foreign Affairs lodged written protest with Japanese Embassy in Nanking. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 17** China sent a memorandum to powers interested in Far East on Japan's latest provocation. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937** Generalissimo Chiang laid down four minimum conditions for settlement of Lukouchiao "incident" in Kuling speech. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 25** Japanese Army sent ultimatum to General Sung Cheh-yuang, Chinese commander in Hopei-Chahar area, demanding evacuation of Chinese troops from Peiping and its vicinity. Gen. Sung ordered his 29th Army to resist. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 27** Japanese troops attacked Peiping. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 28** Chinese troops evacuated Peiping during night. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 29** Fighting broke out in Tientsin. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 July 31** Chinese troops evacuated Tientsin. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Aug. 6** Japan ordered evacuation of Japanese nationals from Hankow and transfer of Japanese Concession in Hankow to Chinese authorities. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Aug. 13** Fighting broke out at Shanghai. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937** National Government announced that Yangtze river below Chinkiang had been mined and closed to shipping. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Aug. 14** Chinese Air Force in action on Shanghai front. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Aug. 21** Treaty of Non-aggression signed in Nanking between China and U.S.S.R. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Aug. 26** Japanese planes bombed and machined-gunned car of British Ambassador Sir Hughe Knatchbull-Hugessen near Wusih, Kiangsu. Sir Hughe wounded in back. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Aug. 27** Chinese troops evacuated Kalgan, capital of Chahar. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Aug. 30** National Government ordered drafting of able-bodied male citizens for military service. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937** China sent statement to the League of Nations on development of events since July 7. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Sept. 1** National Government issued CNC$500,000,000 Liberty Bonds. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Sept. 5** Japanese Navy began blockade of China Coast from Chinwangtao, in north, to Pakhoi, in south. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Sept. 10** "Chinese Red Army" reorganized as part of the Chinese national army. — p. 103 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Sept. 15** China appealed to League Assembly against Japanese aggression under Article XVII of League Covenant. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Sept. 22** Chinese Communist party renounced all communistic programs, pledged full support to Three People's Principles. Also announced abolition of "Chinese Soviet Republic" and "Red Army." — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Sept. 24** Generalissimo Chiang at interview with foreign press declared China was fighting for her existence, also for principles of the Nine-Power Treaty, the Anti-War Pact, and the Covenant of the League of Nations. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Oct. 5** National Government in mandate ordered postponement of National Assembly scheduled for November 12, 1937. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Oct. 6** Assembly of League of Nations adopted Advisory Committee's resolution pledging moral support to China. Japan pronounced guilty of invading China in violation of her treaty obligations. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937** U. S. State Department issued statement condemning Japan's invasion of China, upholding the principles and sanctity of treaties and deploring the use of force. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Oct. 14** Kweisui fell to Japanese. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Oct. 16** National Government accepted invitation to attend the Nine-Power Conference in Brussels on October 30. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Oct. 17** Paotow fell. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Oct. 27** Japan formally declined invitation to attend Nine-Power Conference. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Oct. 29** Puppet Mongol state, called "Autonomous Government of Inner Mongolia," established by Japanese. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Nov. 3** Nine-Power Conference opened in Brussels. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Nov. 5** Japan, Germany and Italy signed Anti-Comintern Pact. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Nov. 9** Chinese troops evacuated Taiyuan. Chinese troops began evacuation of Shanghai area. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Nov. 12** Evacuation of Shanghai completed. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Nov. 20** National Government announced removal from Nanking to Chungking. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Nov. 24** With Italy dissenting, the Nine-Power Conference adopted report and declaration, urging suspension of hostilities and resort to peaceful settlement, and then adjourned indefinitely. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Dec. 12** The U.S.S. Panay, Yangtze river gun-boat, bombed and sunk by Japanese planes near Hohsien, Anhwei. H.M.S. Ladybird and H.M.S. Bee shelled by Japanese artillery near Wuhu. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Dec. 13** Japanese troops entered Nanking. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Dec. 14** Japanese puppet regime, called "Provisional Government of the Republic of China" established in Peiping. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Dec. 24** Hangchow fell. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Dec. 27** Tsinan fell. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1937 Dec. 31** Tsingtao, port city of Shantung, evacuated. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Jan. 1** Government changes enabled Generalissimo to devote himself entirely to military affairs with Dr. H. H. Kung as head of the Executive Yuan. Ministry of Navy abolished, Ministry of Economic Affairs established, and Ministry of Railways merged with Ministry of Communications. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Jan. 16** Japan announced Imperial Conference decision January 11, that Japan would henceforth refuse to deal with National Government. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Jan. 20** Hsu Shih-ying, Chinese Ambassador to Japan, left Japan for China. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Feb. 2** The League Council recommended that members of League individually extend aid to China. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Feb. 20** Hitler announced Germany's decision to recognize puppet regime in Chinese Northeastern Provinces. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Feb. 23** Chinese planes bombed Taiwan. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Feb. 25** Chinese Government protested to Berlin against Germany's decision to recognize puppet regime in Northeastern Provinces. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Mar. 1** First Sino-Soviet Credit Loan of US$50-million concluded. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Mar. 14** National Government banned private foreign exchange operations. Sales of foreign exchange centralized through Central Bank of China. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Mar. 28** New puppet regime under Japanese, called "Reformed Government of the Republic of China," established in Nanking. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Mar. 29** Emergency National Congress of Kuomintang opened in Wuchang, closed April 1. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Apr. 1** Emergency National Congress elected Generalissimo Chiang Tsungtsai of Kuomintang, and program of armed resistance and national reconstruction adopted. Decision to form Sin Min Chu I Youth Corps and People's Political Council. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Apr. 6** Fourth Plenary Session of Central Executive Committe of Kuomintang opened at Wuchang, closed on April 8. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Apr. 7** Gen. Li Tsung-jen reported victory at Taierhchwang. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 May 1** The Ministry of Finance issued 1938 National Defense Loan amounting to CNC$500-million and 1938 Gold Loan amounting to 100-million Customs Gold Units (1 CGU=CNC$20), £10-million and US$50-million. — p. 104 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 May 6** National Government lodged formal protest with Great Britain against Anglo-Japanese agreement on Chinese Maritime Customs. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 May 12** Japanese troops occupied Amoy. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938** Germany formally recognized Japanese puppet regime in Northeastern Provinces. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 May 14** The 101st Session of League of Nations Council adopted resolution urging League members to give serious and sympathetic consideration to requests for aid they might receive from China. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 May 19** Chinese troops evacuated Hsuchow. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 May 20** Chinese planes flew over western Japan and dropped leaflets, the first wartime visit of Japan proper by foreign military planes. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 May 21** Hitler ordered German military advisers serving with Chinese Army to return to Germany. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 June 6** Chinese troops evacuated Kaifeng. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 June 7** Yellow river dikes collapsed below Chengchow as result of hostilities. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 June 11** Chinese Embassy in Tokyo closed by order of National Government. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 June 12** Chinese troops evacuated Anking. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 July 1** Second Sino-Soviet Credit Loan of US$50-million concluded. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 July 3** French Government announced occupation of Paracel islands, Chinese possession south of Hainan island. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 July 6** First session of People's Political Council opened at Hankow. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 July 7** Chinese Ambassador to France, Wellington Koo, reminded French Foreign Minister Georges Bonnet of Chinese sovereignty over Paracel islands. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 July 13** Program of Armed Resistance and National Reconstruction approved by People's Council. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 July 26** Kiukiang fell. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Sept. 11** China officially invoked Article XVII of League Covenant. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Sept. 25** Chinese Delegation at Geneva instructed to demand application of Article XVI of League Covenant. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Sept. 30** League Council adopted report urging member states to abstain from any action that would weaken China and to consider individually measures to aid China. Members might act according to stipulations of Article XVI of Covenant. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Oct. 12** Japanese troops landed at Bias Bay off Kwangtung coast. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938** Sinyang, in southern Honan, fell. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Oct. 21** Japanese troops entered Canton. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Oct. 25** Chinese troops evacuated Wuchang and Hankow. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Oct. 27** Japanese Privy Council confirmed Japanese Government decision to sever relations with League of Nations. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Oct. 28** Second Session of People's Political Council opened at Chungking, closed November 6. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Nov. 13** Chinese troops evacuated Yochow, northern Hunan. Huge fire broke out at Changsha. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Dec. 18** Wang Ching-wei deserted National Government and left Chungking. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Dec. 22** Japanese Prime Minister Prince Konoye in a statement laid down three points as guiding principles for settlement of Sino-Japanese conflict and establishment of "New Order in East Asia." — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Dec. 26** Generalissimo Chiang reiterated China's determination to carry on war of resistance against Japan. He said Konoye's statement clearly revealed Japan's intention to conquer China. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1938 Dec. 29** Wang Ching-wei in Hongkong urged "peace" based on Konoye's three-point statement. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Jan. 1** Wang Ching-wei expelled from Kuomintang. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939** Sikang provincial government formally established at Kangting with General Liu Wen-hui as governor. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Jan. 20** League Council adopted resolution requesting member states to hold consultations for taking effective measures to assist China. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Jan. 21** Fifth Plenary Session of Kuomintang CEC opened at Chungking, closed January 30. Decided to create Supreme National Defense Council under chairmanship of Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Feb. 2** Sino-American Tung Oil Loan of US$25-million concluded. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Feb. 10** Japanese forces invaded Hainan Island. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Feb. 11** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek declared Japanese occupation of Hainan Island marked turning point in history of the Pacific as beginning of Japan's naval dominance in that ocean. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Feb. 12** Third Session of People's Political Council opened in Chungking. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Mar. 8** British Chancellor of the Exchequer, Sir John Simon, announced the grant by British banks guaranteed by the British Government, of £5-million credit to China to stabilize Chinese currency. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Mar. 15** First Sino-British Export Credit Loan, £188,000 concluded. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Mar. 28** Nanchang fell. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Mar. 31** Japanese forces occupied Spratley islands in South China Sea. — p. 105 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 May 1** National Spiritual Mobilization Movement initiated. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 May 3** Chungking heavily bombed by Japanese planes. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 May 4** Chungking heavily bombed. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 May 12** Japanese marines landed at Kulangsu near Amoy. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939** Chungking heavily bombed. May 3, 4 and 12 bombings caused more than 10,000 casualties. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 May 27** League Council urged member states to continue to aid China individually, and asked that the League be kept informed of Japanese bombings of civilian population in China. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 June 1** The 1939 War Supplies Loan of CNC$600-million floated. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 June 8** National Government ordered arrest and punishment of Wang Ching-wei. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 June 10** Third Sino-Soviet Credit Loan, US$150-million, was concluded. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 June 14** Japanese began blockade of British and French concessions in Tientsin. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 June 16** New Sino-Soviet Commercial Treaty signed in Moscow. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 June 18** Remains of Genghis Khan removed from Suiyuan to Kansu. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 June 21** Japanese troops landed at Swatow. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 July 15** Anglo-Japanese negotiations on Tientsin issue began in Tokyo. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 July 24** Generalissimo Chiang stated that Anglo-Japanese accord on China reached in Tokyo would not be valid without China's approval. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 July 26** United States notified Japanese Government of decision to abrogate the American-Japanese Treaty of Commerce and Navigation. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Aug. 14** Anglo-Japanese negotiations in Tokyo on Tientsin issue discontinued. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Aug. 18** Second Sino-British Export Credit Loan, £2,859,000 concluded. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Aug. 23** Pandit Nehru, Indian National Congress Leader, arrived in Chungking on goodwill visit and left on September 5. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Sept. 8** Joint Board of Four Government Banks established with Generalissimo Chiang as chairman. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Sept. 9** Fourth Session of People's Political Council opened in Chungking, closed September 18. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939** Generalissimo Chiang gave reassurance that China would establish a constitutional government. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Oct. 6** Chinese troops won First Battle of Changsha. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Nov. 12** Sixth Plenary Session of Kuomintang CEC opened in Chungking, closed November 20. Generalissimo Chiang appointed president of the Executive Yuan. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1939 Nov. 24** Nanning fell. — p. 106 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))
