**1945** U. S. Marines landed at Tientsin. — p. 113 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 6** Maj.-Gen. Keller E. Rockey, commander of U. S. Marines in north China, accepted surrender of Japanese forces in Tientsin area on behalf of Generalissimo Chiang, supreme commander. — p. 113 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 10** President Truman greeted people of China on 34th Anniversary of Chinese Republic and pledged American assistance to accomplish "democratic objectives" of Dr. Sun. — p. 113 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** U. S. Secretary of State Byrnes announced he had invited powers interested in Far East to send delegates to Washington for meeting scheduled for October 23 to form an Allied Advisory Commission. (Opening meeting postponed until October 30.) — p. 113 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 11** A joint Government-Communist declaration announced agreement had been reached after six weeks of negotiations, unsolved problems to be settled by Political Consultation Conference. — p. 113 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 14** Chinese forces landed on Hainan island to disarm Japanese. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 17** Chungking announced Russian forces had begun leaving Manchuria and complete withdrawal should be effected by end of November. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 22** Lieut.-Gen. Albert C. Wedemeyer, U. S. commander in China, reported in Washington that U. S. troops in China would be cut to 6,000 by January 1, but 53,000 marines would help China repatriate some four million Japanese. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** Returns of plebiscite in Outer Mongolia indicated overwhelming vote in favor of independence. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 28** Fighting between government and communist forces spread to 11 provinces. Government compromise proposal rejected. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 29** Chungking reported Chinese Red troops entrenched in Manchuria along rail lines and behind three ports where U. S. warships were scheduled to land national troops. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Oct. 31** Gen. Yen Hsi-shan, Shansi governor, reported 100,000 communists attacking Tatung. Government's offer not to fire on Reds, if they withdrew from railroads and allowed national troops to pass, rejected by Yenan. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 1** Announcement made that in accordance with terms of Chinese-Soviet treaty, Soviet troops would begin withdrawal from Manchuria November 2 and complete the withdrawal by December 1. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 2** Chungking reported 20,000 Chinese Communist troops led by Gen. Ho Lung were besieging Kweisui. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** Chungking said Chinese Government had requested creation of a five-year U. S. military mission to train and develop China's armed services. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 3** Information Minister K. C. Wu announced that a four-point proposal was sent to Yenan. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 4** Gen. Ho Ying-chin in Peiping announced government decision to reopen rail lines through communist-held areas. Reds attacked north Hupeh cities. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** President Chiang declared support for a free Korea on the eve of President Kim Koo's departure for Seoul. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 5** Information Minister Wu branded as "groundless" charges of Yenan radio and communist paper in Chungking that American armed assistance to Goverment forces was aimed at Reds. Gen. Wedemeyer denied "clashes" between U. S. Marines and communists. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** Chinese Government submitted to Allied Headquarters a list of 300 Japanese war criminals headed by Emperor Hirohito and including every Japanese premier and foreign minister for past 8 years. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 7** Gen. Wedemeyer in Peiping and Secretary Byrnes in Washington announced U. S. Marines would be recalled soon. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 8** Soviet troops withdrew from Hulutao and Yingkow, leaving Chinese Reds in control of ports, while national forces remained on U. S. ships offshore. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 10** Government troops reported in clashes with communists near Shanhaikwan. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 11** Chungking disclosed Chinese-Soviet negotiations permitted flying government troops into Manchuria since communists barred land and sea routes. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 13** Chungking said Russia agreed to let government fly 1,500 troops a day into Changchun, Manchurian capital, from Peiping, and Soviet forces were withdrawing toward Vladivostok, northern Korea and Port Arthur. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** Chungking reported communist troops surrounded airfields evacuated by Russians, making landing of government troops by air transport impossible. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 16** Gen. Tu Yu-ming, commander of government forces, reported capture of Shanhaikwan. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 17** Communist forces continued to besiege Paotow and Kweisui. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 18** Government forces captured Kuimen pass along the Great Wall and pushed on to Chinhsien. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 22** Government troops captured Lienshan, 10 miles from Hulutao, and lifted two-week communist siege of Paotow. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 23** Government troops penetrated 130 miles into Manchuria, half way to Mukden. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 25** Government forces captured Chienshien after 30-mile advance. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 26** President Chiang declared at the newly formed Supreme Economic Council: "We shall spare no effort to bring internal order and security to the nation." He revealed 1,000,000 Japanese still in China. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 27** Maj.-Gen. Patrick J. Hurley resigned as Ambassador to China. Gen. George C. Marshall appointed U. S. President's special envoy to China with ambassadorial rank. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** Chungking reported Russians agreed to help government forces take over Manchuria and had ordered Chinese Communists out of Changchun and Mukden. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Nov. 28** Far Eastern Advisory Commission announced its decision to visit Japan at end of year. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 1** Soviet Union postponed withdrawal of troops from Manchuria until January 3. — p. 114 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 2** Government troops reached Tahushan, 65 miles southwest of Mukden, in drive into Manchuria. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 6** Government forces halted within 25 miles of Mukden, awaiting negotiations with Soviet Marshal Rodion Y. Malinovsky at Changchun. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 7** Over 60 percent of Japanese troops in China were disarmed, Gen. Ho Ying-chin, commander-in-chief of Chinese Army, announced. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 8** 250 officials in the Japanese-sponsored "North China Government" in Peiping were put under arrest. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 10** Attack by 120,000 communists on Lincheng on Tientsin-Pukow railway in Shantung for 13 days. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 11** President and Mme. Chiang visited Peiping for first time in ten years. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 12** Lieut.-Gen. Wedemeyer reported 943,000 Japanese soldiers disarmed in China Theater. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 15** President Truman issued statement on U. S. policy in China, calling for end of civil strife in China and united government under President Chiang. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 17** Seven communist delegates arrived in Chungking to resume negotiations with the government. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 18** Siege of Paotow and Kweisui lifted after two months of unsuccessful assaults by communists. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** President Chiang arrived by plane in Nanking from Peiping. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 22** Gen. George C. Marshall, U. S. Special Envoy, arrived in Chungking by plane from Nanking, and was met by Premier T. V. Soong and Chou En-lai, communist representative. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 23** President and Mme. Chiang returned to Chungking after recent visit to Peiping and Nanking. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** Gen. Marshall conferred with communist leaders. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 25** Gen. Tu Yu-ming, commander of government forces in the northeast, began drive to clear Jehol Province of communist troops. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 27** Government and communist delegates resumed discussion of cessation of hostilities and convening of Political Consultation Conference. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** Big three Foreign Ministers conference announced series of agreements on the establishment of international control of atomic energy; creation of Far Eastern Commission and Allied Council for Japan; ultimate reestablishment of a Free Korea; and withdrawal of Soviet and U. S. troops from China. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 29** Lieut-Gen. Wedemeyer announced U. S. troops would help government forces move into Manchuria. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945 Dec. 31** Government answered communist truce proposal with three-point plan, proposing Gen. Marshall as mediator. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1945** President Chiang offered other political parties, besides Kuomintang, representation in government but decried autonomous armies. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 1** Chinese Navy was placed under direction of the Army. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 2** Government troops entered Jehol province. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 3** Communists accepted government's proposal to make Gen. Marshall mediator in peace negotiations. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 4** Communist party agreed in principle with government that hostilities should be stopped simultaneously with restoration of communications. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 5** China recognized independence of Outer Mongolian Republic. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Partial peace settlement granting Kazakhs high degree of autonomy was reached between government and Kazakhs of Sinkiang province. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 7** Government and communist leaders held first truce meeting with Gen. Marshall acting as mediator. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 10** Cease-fire agreement between Government and communist representatives was announced at Political Consultation Conference (P.C.C.). In opening speech, President Chiang announced government's decision on civil liberties, equality of political parties, local self-government and release of political prisoners. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 11** Second P.C.C. session was held with Chang Chun and Chou En-lai, government and communist representatives, respectively, reporting on newly concluded truce discussions. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 14** Government forces completed occupation of Sinmin, 31 miles northwest of Mukden. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 15** Chang Chun announced setting up of three-man military sub-committee, which held its first meeting on reorganization of communist 18th Group Army and its garrison areas. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 16** U. S. transports moved 6,000 New 6th Army men into Manchuria. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 20** Mme. Chiang arrived in Peiping enroute to northeast on goodwill mission. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 21** National Military Council spokesman said hostilities continued on same scale as before the January 10 truce. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 23** Gen. Marshall accepted advisory position on military sub-committee for studying unification of government and communist armies. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Chinese delegation to United Nations Chief-of-Staff Conference, led by Gen. Shang Chen, former head of Chinese Military Mission in U. S., left for London. — p. 115 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 27** Democratic League members withdrew from P.C.C. session in protest against police search in home of one of their delegates. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 28** Supreme National Defense Council passed resolution to repeal all laws and decrees restricting fundamental freedoms of people. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Jan. 31** P.C.C. reached complete agreement on government reorganization on the basis of coalition of political parties. Among important resolutions adopted at final session: expansion of State Council, nationalization and reorganization of armies, appointment of special committee to review Draft Constitution. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 5** President Chiang disclosed he had not entertained thought of being a presidential candidate under new regime. Government took over administration of the Pescadores. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 11** Washington and London published text of secret Yalta agreement, signed February 11, 1945, by Roosevelt, Churchill and Stalin. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** The Executive Hdqrs. in Peiping, composed of government, communist and U. S. representatives, announced Government-Communist agreements to restore eight disrupted railways in north China. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** President Chiang paid first visit to Shanghai in eight years. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 13** China and Mongolian People's Republic concluded amity pact. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Three-man Military Subcommittee held first formal meeting. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 15** Military Rehabilitation Conference convoked by Chinese Army Hdqrs. opened in Nanking. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Washington disclosed U. S. had asked Chungking and Moscow in identical notes for information on current Chinese-Soviet talks. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Communists in Yenan demanded joint control of Manchuria and limitation of government troops sent there. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 17** Gen. Tu Yu-ming's forces of the 6th and 13th armies captured Liaochung near Mukden. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 19** Quo Tai-chi was appointed Chinese delegate to United Nations Security Council. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 21** Chinese students of 14 educational institutions in and around Chungking started strike in protest against Soviet failure to withdraw from Manchuria. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 22** Demonstration by 30,000 Chungking students against Russia and Chinese Communists. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 23** Seven members of Control Yuan protested to Foreign Affairs Ministry against Yalta agreement. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Students in Chungking staged another demonstration against Soviet delay in withdrawing troops from Manchuria. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 24** President Chiang returned to Chungking from Shanghai by plane after 14-day tour of Shanghai, Nanking and Hangchow. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** A manifesto by 110 professors of National Southwest Associated University in Kunming demanded publication of current negotiations between the Chinese and Soviet governments and the fulfillment of provisions of Chinese-Soviet treaty. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 25** Agreement reached by Military Sub-committee for army reorganization and nationalization of communist troops signed. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Supreme National Defense Council passed provisional regulations governing foreign exchange transactions. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** President Chiang announced government's three principles in negotiations with Soviet Government. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 26** Secretary Byrnes denied knowledge of Big Three agreement that authorized Soviet removal of machinery from Manchuria. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Soviet Supreme military command in northeast announced that Soviet forces would complete evacuation before departure of U. S. troops from China, "or, at any rate, not later than that." — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 27** China and France signed treaty for relinquishment of French extraterritorial and related rights in China, and agreement on relations in Indo-China. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Feb. 28** Gen. Marshall, Gen. Chang Chih-chung and Chou En-lai of Military Sub-committee arrived in Peiping on inspection tour of north China. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 1** Second Plenary Session of Kuomintang 6th Central Executive and Supervisory committee opened in Chungking. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 4** Central Bank of China fixed foreign exchange rate at CN$2,020 to US$1. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 5** U. S. decided to lend China 100 Liberty ships and 100 landing craft to help repatriate Japanese war prisoners and civilians in China. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh told Kuomintang meeting China had rejected Soviet claim to all enterprises in Manchuria that had served Japanese Kwantung army. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 6** Information Minister K. C. Wu announced abolition of press censorship in recovered areas. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Liu Chieh, vice-minister of foreign affairs, stated that delay in Soviet withdrawal was due to technical difficulties which Soviet authorities did not specify. — p. 116 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 7** One million people in Hunan reported facing starvation, lack of clothing, and proper medical care. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 8** Minister of Communications Yu Fei-peng announced government's program to build 20,000 kms. of highways in three stages beginning this year. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Executive Hdqrs. in Peiping ordered communists in Jehol, who violated truce agreements, to withdraw to northwest of Chihfeng within 24 hours. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 9** Chungking reported 22 northbound trains loaded with Soviet troops had left Mukden since March 7. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 10** Executive Yuan instructed Chinese Maritime Customs to permit foreign vessels to sail freely into Chinese harbors until March 31, 1947. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 11** Soviet authorities officially notified Chinese Government of withdrawal of Red troops from Mukden. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Gen. Marshall left Chungking for U. S. to report to President Truman. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 12** Gen. Chang Chih-chung reported to Kuomintang C.E.C. contents of agreement granting self-government to Kazakhs in Sinkiang. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Chinese forces entered Mukden to assume garrison duties following evacuation of Soviet troops. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Food Minister Hsu Kan declared UNRRA deliveries to China not up to schedule and China's food problem critical. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 13** Central News Agency reported Chinese Communists set up puppet regimes in Kirin, Heilungkiang and Hokiang provinces, turning northeast into special area. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Chinese troops moved into Mukden, following complete withdrawal of Soviet forces from city. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 16** Kuomintang C.E.C. unanimously ratified P.C.C. agreements and adopted important resolutions on military, rehabilitation, relief, communications and political problems. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 17** President Chiang obtained Kuomintang CEC ratification of all agreements for reorganization of government and cooperation with communists. At his insistence a resolution condemning negotiations with the U.S.S.R. was defeated. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 19** Lieut.-Gen. Wedemeyer said U. S. should assist China in repatriation of Japanese war prisoners and civilians in Manchuria. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Chinese Communists threatened to break unity agreement, accusing Kuomintang of failing to relinquish control over government. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 20** People's Political Council held first meeting since V-J Day; main item on agenda was national reconstruction. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** U. S. Export-Import Bank granted China US$33-million cotton credit and authorized in principle a slightly larger loan with which to buy ships and machinery. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 22** In accordance with government regulations governing foreign exchange, Central Bank of China designated 27 banks in Shanghai to handle foreign exchange transactions. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 24** Moscow confirmed announcement that U.S.S.R. would complete withdrawal of Red troops from Manchuria by end of April. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 26** Finance Minister O. K. Yui announced national budget of CNC$2,524,900,000,000 at People's Political Council. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 29** Lieut.-Gen. Wedemeyer, on behalf of U. S., presented Generalissimo Chiang the Distinguished Service Medal. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Mar. 30** Government awarded Lieut.-Gen. Wedemeyer Order of Blue-Sky-White-Sun for meritorious service. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 1** President Chiang told P.P.C. that Chinese Communist demands in Manchuria would not be considered until government completed taking-over control from Russians. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Lieut.-Gen. Wedemeyer announced U. S. Army in China Theater would disband on May 1. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 2** Withdrawal of Soviet troops from Harbin, Manchuria, reported in progress. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 3** An estimated 15-million Chinese on the verge of starvation. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** P.P.C. closed session with five-point resolution on agreements of Political Consultation Conference. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 7** Chungking announced schedule of Soviet evacuation of Manchuria. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Government New First Army reported within 70 miles of Changchun. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Two truce-enforcement teams began work of halting Government-Communist conflicts in Manchuria. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 9** Chinese Communist paper made violent personal attack on President Chiang. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 11** President Truman reported that Gen. Marshall's conferences in Washington had been very successful and that General Marshall would return to China at once. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** Chinese Communists were reported concentrated in Harbin and Changchun regions. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))

**1946** U. S. Army personnel in China reduced to 5,000 to serve with American Military Advisory Group. — p. 117 ([scan part 1, leaf 60](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n60/mode/1up))


## Chronology of Major Events — Chronology of Major Events (1911-1949)

**1946 Apr. 12** Death of four leading Chinese Communists in Yenan-bound U. S. Army plane on April 8 was confirmed; casualties included eight other members of Yenan delegation to Chungking parley and American crew of 4. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 12** Chen Kung-po, No. 2 puppet, was sentenced to death. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 13** Government offered to limit number of troops in Manchuria if Chinese Communists would withhold forces, clear Mukden-Peiping rail line and permit government armies to occupy Changchun and Harbin. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 15** Communist Gen. Chou En-lai announced "all-out" hostilities in Manchuria. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 15** UNRRA estimated more than 30 million Chinese starving in 19 provinces; urged quick relief. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 15** New First Army clashed with communist forces in Szepingkai in drive toward Changchun. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 16** Two hours before Soviet army completed withdrawal from Changchun, Chinese Communists opened heavy attack on Manchurian capital, seizing three airfields. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 17** Chinese Communists captured Changchun. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 18** Gen. Marshall, accompanied by his wife, arrived in Chungking. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 22** Chu Min-yi, puppet foreign minister, was sentenced to death; Mrs. Wang Ching-wei, widow of puppet president, was given life term for treason. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 24** French Goodwill Mission arrived in Nanking from Peiping. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 25** President Chiang postponed National Assembly when communists refused to nominate candidates for government posts. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 25** Chinese Communists submitted to Gen. Marshall three proposals as conditions for signing truce. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 26** Chinese Communist troops took over Harbin as Russians left. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 27** Generalissimo Chiang personally took part in truce negotiations for a speedy settlement, conferred with Gen. Marshall. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 28** Chinese Reds controlled Tsitsihar after Russian withdrawal. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 30** UNRRA began on final 60-day engineering project to close Yellow river gap before July 1 high flood deadline. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Apr. 30** Truce negotiations on Manchuria collapsed when President Chiang rejected latest communist demands. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 1** Government officially moved back to Nanking. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 1** Herbert Hoover, President Truman's food investigator, arrived in Shanghai. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 2** Gen. Marshall urged Chinese Reds to hand over Changchun to government. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 3** Government rejected three-point Manchurian peace proposal by Gen. Marshall. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 5** Cease-fire team hurried to Hankow where Government-Communist battle flared. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 6** Delayed dispatch from Changchun indicated Chinese Reds claimed control over 70 percent of Manchuria, with local regimes established in most provinces. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 10** New truce in central China announced, paving way for further efforts to settle Manchurian conflict. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 11** Chinese Government-U. S. Army agreement for repatriation of 1,300,000 Japanese settlers from Manchuria signed in Mukden. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 12** Government-Communist truce concluded in Shantung. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 13** Gen. Ho Ying-chin resigned as chief of staff of armed forces. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 14** Col. R. H. Harrison, President Truman's personal representative in China, reported to UNRRA in Shanghai that Hunan situation had reached critical plight. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 15** Government changes: Gen. Pai Chung-hsi, assistant chief of staff, named head of new Ministry of National Defense; Chen Cheng, Minister of War, named chief of staff to replace Gen. Ho Ying-chin; Yu Ta-wei named Minister of Communications to succeed Gen. Yu Fei-peng; Peng Hsueh-pei named Minister of Information; K. C. Wu appointed Mayor of Shanghai; Wang Yun-wu named Minister of Economic Affairs to succeed Wong Wen-hao. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 16** Government was advised that all Russian forces had withdrawn from Manchuria except for some troops at Port Arthur and Dairen. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 19** Government and communist sources reported that fighting in Shantung, Hopei and Jehol was developing gradually into large-scale conflict. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 19** Admiral Charles M. Cooke, Jr., commander of U. S. 7th Fleet, said U. S. was not planning to develop naval bases in China to compete with Soviet-controlled Port Arthur. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 20** Headquarters of Lieut.-Gen. Tu Yu-ming, commander of Government forces in Manchuria, announced capture of Szepingkai. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 20** Hunan delegates in National Assembly reported 20-million victims in 54 of 78 famine-stricken cities in Hunan province. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 21** Central News Agency reported Government First Army had pushed to within 35 miles of Changchun. — p. 118 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 22** Moscow radio quoted Tass report that seven American-piloted planes had been shot down in Manchuria while bombing Chinese Communists. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 22** Government forces were reported to have captured communist stronghold of Kungchuling and pushed to within 20 miles of Changchun. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 23** Government announced recapture of Changchun, held by Chinese Communists since April 18. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 23** President Chiang left Nanking for Mukden. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 23** U. S. Army and Marine Corps denied Moscow report on downing of American piloted planes in Manchuria. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 26** Government troops were reported near Harbin. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 27** Government and Chinese Communists resumed truce negotiations. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 27** Government planned to complete repatriation of Japanese war prisoners and civilians before end of June. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 29** Spokesman declared government still insistent on establishment of Chinese sovereignty throughout Northeast Provinces. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 29** Government troops captured Kirin east of Changchun. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 30** Gen. Marshall warned China was on "the verge of an even greater calamity" than World War. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 30** New armed forces commanders appointed: Army, Ku Chu-tung; Navy, Chen Cheng, chief of staff; and Air Force, Chou Chih-jou. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 May 31** Government troops in Manchuria advanced to Sungari river defense line, 76 miles south of Harbin. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 1** Gen. Chang Chih-chung, director of President Chiang's headquarters for the northwest, concluded agreement in Sinkiang with representatives of Moslem Turki tribes. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 2** Gen. Marshall proposed new plan for stopping civil war in Manchuria through Government-Communist truce. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 3** Chen Kung-po, former Nanking puppet president, executed. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 4** President Chiang returned to Nanking from the northeast. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 4** Communists claimed recapture of Anshan and Yingkow in Manchuria. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 5** President Chiang agreed with Gen. Marshall to halt government offensive for ten days for negotiation with Chinese Communists. Advance section of Sino-American Executive Headquarters in Peiping was ordered to proceed to Changchun to prepare for "carrying out whatever agreements may be reached for the cessation of hostilities" in Manchuria. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 6** President Chiang ordered national troops in Manchuria to halt "all advances, attacks, and pursuits" during 15-day armistic, as substitute for full truce. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 8** Chinese minority parties sought to prolong Manchuria truce with communists, which was to end June 22. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 8** Spokesman of Government Field Headquarters charged communist troops violated 15-day truce agreement within three hours after it came into effect. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 10** Moscow radio broadcast Tass dispatch from Shanghai charging U. S. naval and marine units had been "authorized to render every possible assistance to Chinese Government troops operating against communist troops in Manchuria." — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 11** T. F. Tsiang, CNRRA head, announced government appropriation of CNC$432-million, 50 percent of nation's revenue, for CNRRA operation in 1946. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 11** Semi-official report indicated Communist Gen. Chou En-lai demanded communist participation in government as price for peace. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 12** Sinkiang provincial government ordered release of more than 300 political prisoners arrested in connection with Ining incident. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 14** President Truman told Congress that China had received $602,045,000 in lend-lease aid from U. S. since V-J Day and that the assistance was to continue. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 16** Chinese Communist spokesman announced signing of new truce, an implementation agreement described as a "step toward a permanent cease-fire in Manchuria." — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 17** Edwin W. Pauley's reparations mission reported Manchurian industry had been crippled by Soviet removal of vital machinery. Soviet government did not "make exactly a gesture of good-will toward China or other Allied nations entitled to reparations from Japan." — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 18** Executive Yuan promulgated law giving Sinkiang people voice in administration of province under Urumchi agreement. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 19** U. S. Acting Secretary of State Dean Acheson declared reorganization of Chinese Army with help of 1,000 U. S. Army-Navy experts proposed by Gen. Marshall would have far-reaching results in rehabilitation of China. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 21** President Chiang extended 15-day truce until June 30 to give Government-Communist negotiators more time to settle differences. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 22** Mao Tse-tung demanded U. S. to cease all military aid to Chinese Government and promptly withdraw U. S. forces from China. — p. 119 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 25** U. S. State Department indicated U. S. intended to continue policy of military aid to China at least as long as Japanese troops remained there. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 26** American representatives on cease-fire teams in Manchuria were given added power under Government-Communist agreement. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 26** V. K. Wellington Koo was appointed Ambassador to U. S. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 29** U. S. State Department issued notice that U. S. guns would go to China if and when its Government and communist armies were merged into a single Chinese Army. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 June 30** Government announced that the twice-extended truce in Manchuria was to be prolonged indefinitely. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 3** The Supreme National Defense Council voted to convoke the National Assembly on November 12, 1946, to adopt a constitution. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 9** UNRRA Director-General La Guardia announced shipments of supplies other than emergency food to China were suspended until UNRRA goods had been cleared out of coastal ports. President Truman nominated Dr. J. Leighton Stuart U. S. Ambassador to China. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 11** Government spokesman said CNRRA had standing order to distribute its relief supplies fairly without regard to politics. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 12** T. F. Tsiang, CNRRA director, said poor transportation, not politics, handicapped flow of food to China's starving millions. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 13** Seven U. S. Marines guarding munition dumps kidnapped near Chinwangtao by Chinese Communists. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 13** U. S. Army headquarters in Shanghai announced repatriation of Japanese completed in China proper. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 16** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced appointment of Cheng Tien-hsi as Ambassador to Great Britain to succeed V. K. Wellington Koo. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 20** Chinese Government asked UNRRA to resume relief shipments to China, blamed UNRRA itself for delays in the distribution of supplies. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 21** Government troops launched all-out campaign to break communist grip on north China railroads. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 23** Edwin Pauley reported that Soviet removal of key machinery in Manchuria had "incapacitated" more than US$2-billion worth of industry there. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 July 29** Four U. S. Marines killed and 16 seriously injured when truck convoy was ambushed by Chinese Communists 25 miles southeast of Peiping. Four more died after arrival in Peiping. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 2** Communist spokesman declared Yenan would appeal for international volunteer army to fight government forces if present hostilities should become all-out civil war. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 5** U. S. State Department spokesman said U. S. Government had no intention of withdrawing marines now stationed in China. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 9** A new truce until August 26 was announced. The Executive Headquarters ordered communist and government troops in Hupeh, Honan and Shansi provinces to cease fire and withdraw ten miles from their respective positions. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 10** Gen. Marshall and Dr. Stuart, new U. S. Ambassador, declared it "appears impossible" for government and communists to reach settlement on different issues. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 10** U. S. Marine Corps Headquarters announced second clash between marines and Chinese Reds had taken place 60 miles northeast of Tientsin. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 14** President Chiang, on first anniversary of Japanese surrender, promised to end one-party rule without further delay. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 15** Chinese Communists attacked Gen. Marshall, blaming him for failure to cease hostilities in China. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 16** Henry Pu Yi, puppet ruler of "Manchukuo," testified in Tokyo trial. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 18** Government announced new foreign exchange rate of CNC$3,350 to US$1. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 19** Yenan ordered mobilization of all forces for full-scale civil war against Central Government "to shatter Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek's offensive." — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 20** Gen. Chou En-lai, chief communist negotiator, rejected President Chiang's invitation to submit names of communist candidates for participation in a reorganized government. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 21** Communists announced establishment of government in Manchuria under provisional administration of 86 elected delegates. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 23** Gen. Marshall flew to Kuling to discuss with President Chiang on the creation of an interim State Council to be composed of representatives of all parties. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 29** President Chiang approved creation of committee of five, to be headed by U. S. Ambassador J. Leighton Stuart, to pave the way for coalition government. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Aug. 31** T. V. Soong, president of Executive Yuan, announced conclusion of surplus property negotiations with U. S. Government which would enable China to get more than US$800-million worth of U. S. surplus material in China and western Pacific. — p. 120 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 1** Chou En-lai denounced surplus property deal between Chinese and U. S. governments and demanded United States to "suspend assistance to the Kuomintang." — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 4** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh disclosed China would ask a 40 percent share in the total Japanese reparations pool. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 6** Government forces launched two-pronged drive toward Harbin, Manchuria's largest industrial center. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 8** Chou En-lai denied Chinese Communist party had "any connection with Moscow," declaring Yenan would not seek any aid from Russia. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 9** Chinese Embassy in Washington disclosed Chinese Government had permitted foreign vessels to call at Nanking, Wuhu, Kiukiang and Hankow within a specified period of time. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 12** Communists proposed immediate meetings of the Military Sub-committee to work out quick truce to end civil strife. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 16** Chou En-lai left Nanking for Shanghai, ending hopes for a negotiated peace through U. S. Ambassador Stuart's proposed five-man committee. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 15** Government forces drove deep wedges between Yenan and Kalgan. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 17** Government forces captured Huaiyin, main communist base in northern Kiansu. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 18** Government forces entered Tatung, lifting 45-day communist siege. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 23** Andrei Gromyko, Soviet delegate to UN Security Council, said that the presence of U. S. Marines in China might lead to international friction. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 24** Premier Joseph Stalin asserted that earliest withdrawal of U. S. armed forces in China would be vital to futuie peace. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 24** Lieu Chieh, vice-minister of foreign affairs, declared in Nanking that American Marines were in China to assist in the repatriation of Japanese nationals and their presence was with the sanction of the Chinese Government. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 28** Communist forces under Gen. Lin Piao launched surprise offensive in Manchuria, directing an attack against Nungan, railway town 35 miles north of Changchun. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 30** Three powerful Government columns from Hopei, Jehol and Suiyuan converged on Kalgan, principal communist military center in north China. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Sept. 30** Chinese Communist party notified the government of its refusal to attend National Assembly scheduled for Nov. 12. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 4** Communist forces attacked Paoting, capital of Hopei province. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 6** President Chiang accepted proposal of Gen. Marshall and Ambassador Stuart for a 10-day truce to pave way for resumption of peace talks. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 8** Gen. Marshall and Ambassador Stuart declared in a joint statement that Yenan had refused to agree to a 10-day truce on the Kalgan drive and Gen. Chou En-lai had refused to return from Shanghai to resume peace negotiations. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 9** British Trade Mission, headed by Sir Leslie Boyce, arrived in China. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 10** President Chiang urged national unity in Double Tenth message to the Chinese people, emphasizing that "basic conditions for national reconstruction are national unification and social stability." — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 11** Ministry of National Defense announced the capture of Kalgan by Gen. Fu Tso-yi's troops after a year-long communist occupation. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 12** Dr. Sun Fo, president of Legislative Yuan, urged resumption of peace parleys and reorganization of government in accordance with PCC resolutions. He requested all parties to submit immediately names of their delegates to the National Assembly. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 12** Chinese Communists appealed to UN for assistance in effecting withdrawal of U. S. troops from China. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 15** Gen. Fu Tso-yi, conqueror of Kalgan, was appointed concurrently governor of Chahar province. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 15** Troops from Outer Mongolia were reported to have invaded northern Manchuria and entered Hulun, capital of Hsingan province. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 16** President Chiang set forth eight conditions, declaring that should the communists agree to the terms, the government would issue nation-wide cease-fire order. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 16** Third party leaders approved in principle government's latest proposal. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 18** Yenan issued statement through central committee saying communists would reject the government's latest peace offer unless the government agreed to restore military status quo of Jan. 13 and observed PCC resolutions. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 22** Gen. Chou En-lai and third parties delegates returned to Nanking. Preliminary peace discussions took place. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 22** President and Madame Chiang left for Taiwan to attend first anniversary of restoration of the island to China. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 26** Dr. Sun Fo revealed six points of conflict remained to be thrashed out before peace negotiations could officially begin. — p. 121 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 29** President Chiang exhorted third party members to persevere in their peace efforts. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 30** President Chiang offered to issue cease-fire order provided the communists would submit list of delegates to National Assembly. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Oct. 31** Nationwide celebration marked President Chiang's 60th birthday. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 4** U. S. and China signed five-year treaty of friendship, commerce and navigation. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 5** Government forces lifted Paoting siege. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 8** President Chiang instructed government troops throughout country, beginning noon Nov. 11, to cease fire and remain at their respective positions. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 9** William Henry Donald, formerly adviser to President Chiang, died in Shanghai. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 11** Communist spokesman Wang Ping-nan declared communist delegation would leave Nanking if government convened National Assembly as scheduled on Nov. 12. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))
