**1946 Nov. 11** Government decided to postpone National Assembly for three days in last minute effort to find basis for peace with communists. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 15** National Assembly officially convened. President Chiang declared in inaugural speech end of Kuomintang tutelage. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 16** Large communist forces attacked Langfang on Peiping-Tientsin railway. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 17** T. V. Soong announced revised provisional foreign trade regulations giving priority to imports. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 20** Supreme National Defense Council decided to reorganize Executive Yuan to include 18 ministries and commissions and to increase members of State Council to 40. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 21** National Assembly elected 46 members of 55-man presidium from 178 candidates, reserving nine seats for Communist party and Democratic League. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Nov. 27** President Chiang introduced draft constitution to National Assembly. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 2** Dr. Sun Fo, president of Legislative Yuan, declared "the government is ready at all times to resume negotiations with Communists." — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 6** Yenan demanded dissolution of National Assembly and restoration of military positions to status of Jan. 13 as conditions for resumption of peace talks. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 9** Government announced it was considering sending representatives to Yenan to reopen peace negotiations with communists. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 13** Edwin W. Pauley, U. S. reparations representative, in report to President Truman, declared Manchuria's industrial plants had suffered appalling damages totalling US$858-million. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 14** Chou En-lai accused President Chiang of cooperating with Germany and Italy in early days of Sino-Japanese War and U. S. of harboring imperialistic designs of world domination. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 16** Steering Committee of National Assembly, at the request of President Chiang, killed all constitutional amendments proposed by various assembly committees, upheld draft constitution drawn in January by all-party Political Consultation Conference. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 18** President Truman, in 3,500 word statement of U. S. policy in China, reiterated that civil war threatening world peace must be ended; adding that U. S. would continue to respect China's sovereignty and help Chinese toward recovery of peace and economy. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 23** U. S. State Department announced signing of Chinese-American civil aviation agreement under which airlines of one nation would receive reciprocal landing and transit rights in territory of the other. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 25** National Assembly, 41 days after opening session, completed new constitution for China. Assembly rushed through third and final reading of the new national charter and approved it by almost unanimous vote. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 28** Crash of three airliners caused by heavy fog near Shanghai, killed 77 persons and seriously injured 22 others. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 30** More than 6,000 Peiping university students in street demonstration demanding withdrawal of U. S. Marines because of alleged rape of Chinese co-ed by two U. S. marines on Christmas eve. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1946 Dec. 31** President Chiang broadcast reiterated policy of continuing attempts to settle disputes with communists through political means. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 1** In Shanghai 5,000 university students staged demonstration in protest against alleged Peiping rape case. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 3** Marching student demonstrators in Nanking protested alleged Peiping rape case. Students presented list of demands to U. S. Ambassador Stuart. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 4** T. V. Soong, president of Executive Yuan, ordered all local governments and schools to halt anti-American student demonstrations. — p. 122 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 5** Tung Pi-wu, chief communist delegate, charged that the proposed closing of breaches in Yellow river dike, near Kaifeng, threatened vast stretches of communist-controlled territory. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 6** White House announced Gen. George C. Marshall had been summoned to Washington to report on situation in China. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 6** U. S. State Department announced it had officially requested Soviet Union to turn over control of Dairen to China immediately. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 7** Gen. Marshall was appointed American Secretary of State. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 8** Gen. Marshall left Nanking by plane for U. S. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 9** Information Minister Peng Hsueh-pei declared in formal statement government's willingness to discuss with communists concrete plans for cessation of hostilities and government reorganization. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 16** Government named Gen. Chang Chih-chung, governor of Sinkiang province, to visit Yenan in attempt to renew peace negotiations with communists. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 18** Yenan reply rejecting government proposal for new parleys was conveyed to Ambassador Stuart by communists in Nanking. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 21** Government announced its readiness to resume peace talks with communists on basis of four points: cessation of hostilities with troops remaining at their present positions, army reorganization, reopening of communications and a "just and equitable solution of problem of regional administration." — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 25** Charging that by signing the Sino-American Commercial Treaty "the government had sold out the territorial sovereignty of the entire country and the right to existence of the Chinese nation," the communists proclaimed November 4, the day of the signing of the treaty, "National Humiliation Day." — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 29** U. S. State Department announced abandonment of U. S. effort to mediate between Chinese Government and communists. The decision involved ending of U. S. connection with Committee of Three, Executive Headquarters in Peiping, and withdrawal of all U. S. forces from China. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 29** Soviet newspaper Izvestia assailed Edwin W. Pauley's report on Soviet looting in Manchuria as "slanderous." — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Jan. 30** Wang Ping-nan, communist spokesman, announced Yenan's decision to impose its political demands on government by force of arms and continued harassment of communications. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 1** Six thousand Communists cut Peiping-Tientsin railway as they opened new assault 25 miles southeast of Peiping. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 2** Government forces recaptured Tancheng, 28 miles south of communist New 4th Army Headquarters at Lini in Shantung. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 4** President Chiang made second flight in three days to Chengchow. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 5** T. V. Soong, president of Executive Yuan, announced subsidy plan of granting 100 percent bonus to exporters as means of lending immediate stimulus to China's export trade. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 6** U. S. branch of Executive Headquarters formally closed down. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 11** President Chiang ordered drastic measures to break black market and halt inflation. Open market exchange rate closed at CNC$17,500 to one US dollar. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 15** Government forces captured Lini, communist New 4th Army Headquarters in Shantung. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 16** All private transactions in gold and foreign currency were banned. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 18** Government asked permission of UNRRA to sell US$200,000 worth of agricultural and industrial goods earmarked for China to buy cereals, grains and cotton to be sold without restriction on open market. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 20** T. V. Soong and Maj.-Gen. Lowell W. Rooks, Director-General of UNRRA, denied report that China sought permission to sell UNRRA goods on black market. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 21** Twenty communists including Gen. Yeh Chien-yin, communist representative at Executive Headquarters, left Peiping for Yenan. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 22** Government received note from U. S., Great Britain and France, declaring agenda of next month's conference of Big Four Foreign Ministers at Moscow would not go beyond questions of German and Austrian peace treaties without China's consent. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 23** Communists resumed offensive along Sungari river. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 28** Seventy thousand communists drove south from positions above Sungari river to within eight miles of Changchun. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Feb. 28** Rioting broke out in Taipeh, capital of Taiwan, following a clash between police enforcing tobacco monopoly and crowd of several hundred people. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 1** T. V. Soong, president of Executive Yuan, resigned. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 1** Supreme National Defense Council named President Chiang concurrently president of Executive Yuan. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 1** Government broadened its legislative, supervisory and deliberate branches and issued mandate appointing 81 third party representatives to Legislative Yuan, Control Yuan and People's Political Council. — p. 123 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 1** U. S. War Department reported two American army officers were seized by communists in Manchuria. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 1** Tsuyee Pei resigned as governor of Central Bank of China and Chang Kia-ngau was appointed as his successor. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 4** President Chiang announced abolition of Supreme Economic Council and creation of National Economic Council under his chairmanship. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 10** Rioting broke out anew in Taiwan. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 10** Soviet Foreign Minister Molotov proposed addition of Chinese problem on agenda of Big Four Foreign Ministers Conference. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 12** Liu Chieh, vice-minister of foreign affairs, declared "China will not tolerate her internal affairs being discussed by Moscow conference with or without China's participation." — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 12** American liaison group in Yenan returned to Nanking. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 15** Yellow river dike breach at Hwayuankow was closed. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 16** National demonstrations were held against foreign intervention in Chinese affairs. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 19** Government announced capture of Yenan. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 22** Kuomintang CEC convened on March 15, decided to recall and "severely punish" Chen Yi, governor of Taiwan province. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 24** Kuomintang CEC decided to abolish Supreme National Defense Council and create vice-presidency after government reorganizations. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 26** Organic law of National Government was revised by Legislative Yuan. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Mar. 30** Legislative Yuan passed election laws. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 1** Communists launched new offensive in Honan, aiming at capture of Sinsiang. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 1** U. S. Navy authorities in China announced that 5,000 Marines would be withdrawn from China to Guam. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 1** Gen. Pai Chung-hsi, Minister of National Defense, issued statement on Taiwan suggesting that the Taiwan Chief Administrator's Office be changed into a provincial government. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 2** Government forces captured Taian, major communist base on Tsientsin-Pukow railway. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 5** Communists killed five U. S. marines and wounded 16 others in raid on marine ammunition dump near Tangku. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 7** Russia charged that U. S. had failed to withdraw its troops from China. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 8** Government published rules concerning sale of state-operated businesses to individuals. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 9** China supported American-proposed principle of advance drawing of part of available assets in Japan for reparations. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 10** Government decided to distribute U. S. surplus ships secured by China among privately-operated shipping companies. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 14** Chinese Government announced that it reserved rights on treaty with Germany. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 16** Government announced that henceforth all foreign aircraft flying over country must obtain permission from China. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 16** China suggested that consultations be held among U. S., Britain, U.S.S.R. and China on question of Korea. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 17** American Embassy in China denied that Major Robert Riggs and Captain John Colling, assistant military attaches captured by communists, were reconnoitering communist military positions in Manchuria. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 17** Heavy fighting raged near Shihchiachwang, railway town in western Hopei. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 18** Government appointed Gen. Chang Chun president of Executive Yuan and named new state councillors. Dr. Sun Fo was chosen vice-president of National Government. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 19** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced conclusion of new Sino-Philippine treaty of amity. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 19** Gen. Shang Chen was appointed Chinese representative on Allied Council in Japan. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 21** 52 barges loaded with UNRRA relief goods left Tientsin for communist territory. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 22** Wei Tao-ming, former Chinese Ambassador to U. S., was appointed governor of Taiwan province. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 23** Newly formed multi-party State Council met for first time and members of new Executive Yuan were announced. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 23** Government announced dispatching of survey party to Dairen and Port Arthur. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 24** Communist forces on Shantung peninsula were ferried to Dairen to obstruct government occupation of port. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 26** President Truman ordered transfer of unspecified number of surplus ships to China. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 26** Government forces captured Mengyin in Shantung. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 29** Twelve Taiwanese were given high position in new provincial administration. Nepalese Goodwill Mission arrived in Shanghai. — p. 124 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Apr. 30** Taian fell to the communists again. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 1** Premier Chang Chun, reporting on administrative policies at Legislative Yuan, reiterated government's willingness to settle internal strife through political means. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 1** Government announced that foreigners traveling in China would no longer need internal visa. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 2** U. S. Secretary of State Marshall announced China had agreed to begin talks on settling its outstanding war accounts with U. S. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 5** Soviet Ambassador Petrov conveyed Russian reply to Chinese Government's note regarding procedure on taking over Dairen and Port Arthur. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 6** Government troops recaptured Taian. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 7** Admiral Charles M. Cooke, Commander of U. S. 7th Fleet, announced withdrawal of U. S. Marines in Peiping and Tsientsin areas would be completed before July 1. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 8** Finance Minister O. K. Yui announced that issuance of national currency notes up to the end of April totalled about CNC$6,000-billion. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 9** Anyang, 70 miles north of Kaifeng in Honan, beseiged by communists. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 10** Government troops captured Laiwu, southeast of Tsinan. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 18** Chen Chi-tien, secretary-general of Young China party, was appointed minister of economic affairs. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 19** Mai Ssu Wu Teh, Kuomintang CEC member from Sinkiang, succeeded Gen. Chang Chih-chung as governor of Sinkiang. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 26** Third Plenary Session of 4th PPC, convened on May 20, resolved to invite its communist members to attend session. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 May 31** PPC adopted resolution asking government to initiate negotiations with U. S. for loans to be used exclusively for national rehabilitation. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 3** Chinese mission arrived in Port Arthur to investigate conditions in Port Arthur and Dairen. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 5** Outer Mongolian cavalry battalion, aided by four planes with Soviet insignia, attacked Chinese defense forces at Peitashan, northeast of Kitai in eastern Sinkiang. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 11** Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced China had lodged protest with Soviet and Outer Mongolian governments on invasion of Sinkiang. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 12** Communists rejected peace proposals of PPC. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 12** Peitashan was recaptured from Outer Mongolian troops. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 12** U.N. Economic Commission for Asia and Far East met in Shanghai. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 12** CNAC airliner completing first round trip between China and U. S. returned to Shanghai. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 18** Communist troops in Szepingkai, north of Mukden, used poison gas against government forces. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 20** Vice-president Sun Fo declared immediate Anglo-American aid to China would be necessary if Manchuria was to be saved. Communist troops occupied Tsanghsien on Tientsin-Pukow railway. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 22** Outer Mongolian troops renewed attacks on Chinese positions at Peitashan in Sinkiang province. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 22** Heavy fighting continued at Szepingkai, north of Mukden. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 25** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs issued communique on Soviet Union's repeated attempts to block China's entry into Dairen and Port Arthur. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 25** Government forces recaptured Kungchuling, north of Szepingkai. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 27** American State Department announced U. S. would sell 130-million rounds of small-arms ammunition to China at one-tenth of original cost. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 June 27** Outer Mongolia was reported to have replied on June 22 to China's protest regarding Peitashan incident, putting blame on China. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 1** Communist siege of Szepingkai, strategic railway hub north of Mukden, was lifted. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 3** Communist troops were reported to have crossed Yellow river in Shantung from north bank. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 7** Gen. Chen Cheng, chief of general staff, placed total strength of communist forces at more than 1,170,000 men including some 100,000 Japanese prisoners of war. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 8** The Executive Yuan passed emergency measures to cope with flood in Kwangtung, Kwangsi and Fukien provinces. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 9** Outer Mongolian invaders continued attacks on Peitashan, Sinkiang. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 10** Chengteh, capital of Jehol, was recaptured. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 11** China lodged fresh protests with Outer Mongolia. Russia issued communique through Tass denying China's right to take over Dairen and Port Arthur. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 12** President Truman appointed Lieut.-Gen. Albert C. Wedemeyer head of a fact-finding mission to China and Korea. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 17** The Ministry of Interior announced China's population in first half of 1947 at 461,006,285. — p. 125 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 17** Gen. Sun Li-jen was appointed deputy-commander of Chinese Ground Forces. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 18** Government adopted plans to mobilize material and manpower to suppress the communist rebellion. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 22** Wedemeyer mission arrived in Nanking. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 23** T. F. Tsiang, former CNRRA director, was appointed acting Chinese delegate to U.N. Security Council. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 25** PPC standing committee approved Chinese participation in preliminary Japanese peace conference but proposed China must retain veto power. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 28** China turned down Outer Mongolia's bid for U.N. membership. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 July 30** UNRRA announced in Washington suspension of shipments to both government and communist areas in north China. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 1** State Council approved sending a trade mission to Japan. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 7** President Chiang flew to Yenan, former communist capital, to confer on strengthening government's defenses in the northwest. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 11** Government forces captured Yencheng and Chuyeh in southwestern Shantung. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 12** Government rejected UNRRA request to guarantee safety of personnel engaged in delivering relief supplies to communist-held areas. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 13** Premier Chang Chun expressed welcome of foreign investment in China's industrial and manufacturing enterprises. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 14** Government forces lifted communist siege of Yulin in northern Shensi. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 15** U. S. turned down Soviet proposal that Japanese peace treaty be written by governments of United States, Soviet Union, Great Britain and China only. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 16** Open animosity between Moslems loyal to Central Government and local tribes opposed to Chinese authority was reported to have developed in Sinkiang province. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 17** Government announced new foreign exchange policy permitting dealings in foreign exchange by importers and exporters at open market rate; foreign exchange at official rate only for essential imports. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 17** Government forces launched new three-pronged attack to clear communist forces from Tsinan-Tsingtao railway. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 20** Executive Yuan ordered closing of Soviet-controlled port of Dairen to all foreign shipping. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 21** The U. S. State Department protested to USSR over continued Soviet occupation of Dairen and procrastination in making port available to world shipping. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 24** Concluding his study of China situation, Gen. Wedemeyer issued statement stressing need for "drastic far-reaching political and economic reforms" in Chinese Government. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 28** Communist forces under Liu Po-cheng, driven from western Shantung province, cut a wide swath through Honan-Anhwei border area to within 150 miles north of Hankow. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Aug. 31** Gen. Chen Cheng, chief of General Staff, flew to Mukden to take over government command in Manchuria. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 2** Premier Chang Chun declared in an interview that China would not alter her domestic or foreign policy as result of Gen. Wedemeyer's mission. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 4** National Economic Council adopted plan under which all Chinese nationals holding private assets abroad would be required to register such assets with Central Bank or be subject to a maximum penalty of five years' imprisonment. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 5** Executive Yuan announced new program for reduction of government payroll, conservation of food, cloth and gasoline, stricter rationing of newsprint and other measures to promote austerity and increase government efficiency. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 5** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh disclosed China had recommended retaining of veto power by Big Four in working out Japanese peace settlement. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 6** Gen. Hsiung Shih-hui, former director of the Generalissimo's Northeast Headquarters, revealed government capture of Russian arms from Korean units fighting with Chinese Communists. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 7** San Min Chu I Youth Corps, organized in 1938, was due for dissolution before end of month. Its 400,000 members would be absorbed into Kuomintang. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 10** Government reached agreement with U. S. on main principles governing use of China's share of US$350-million U. S. relief appropriation for needy foreign countries. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 13** T. V. Soong was appointed governor of Kwangtung. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 14** Ministry of Foreign Affairs published text of a statement handed to Lieut.-Gen. Wedemeyer on Aug. 16 relating Chinese Government's work and policy before and after the war. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 14** Communist troops were reported roaming on north bank of Yangtze river about fifty miles from Nanking. Government imposed curfew on Nanking suburbs and river traffic. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 16** Legislative Yuan ratified air pact with Britain and peace treaty with Italy. — p. 126 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 19** Chang Chun, president of Executive Yuan, started inspection tour of north China. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 23** Resident Committee of PPC adopted 15-point recommendation on Japanese peace treaty to be submitted to government for deliberation. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 24** Government troops tightened cordon around communist forces on Honan-Hupeh-Anhwei border. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Sept. 26** State Council postponed general election for one month because of delay in preparations in some localities. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 1** Government troops captured Chefoo. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 1** Hollington K. Tong, director of Government Information Office, condemned Democratic League members for taking part in communist activities. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 3** Ministry of Justice reported 22,661 treason cases had been tried. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 4** President and Madame Chiang arrived in Peiping. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 5** Government naval units captured Weihaiwei. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 7** Legislative Yuan ratified Chinese-Canadian agreement on a $6-million (Canadian dollars) loan to China. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 9** British Parliamentary Goodwill Mission arrived in Nanking. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 13** William C. Bullitt, former U. S. Ambassador to Russia, in article in Life magazine, advocated immediate U. S. aid to China. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 17** China donated 2-million c.c. of cholera serum to Egypt and Arabia. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 20** Premier Chang Chun, addressing PPC Resident Committee, declared the Liuchius should be returned to China; disclosing only 9.2 percent of Manchuria's total territory and 29.9 percent of Manchuria's total population were under government control. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 22** Information Director Hollington K. Tong declared government had no intention of reviving press censorship. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 22** President Chiang returned to Nanking after inspection trip to Kiaotung peninsula. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 27** Government outlawed Democratic League on charges of complicity with Chinese Communists. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 27** China signed agreement on distribution of US$30-million post-UNRRA aid. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 27** Yellow River Conservancy Engineering Administration announced that work to rebuild dikes along lower section of the river could not be completed because of communist obstruction. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Oct. 29** Executive Yuan endorsed Reparations Commission's plan to allot one-fourth of first instalment of Japanese reparations to civilian enterprises, three-fourths to government organizations. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 1** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh, referring to China's claims on Japanese reparations, said "China is entitled to a percentage commensurate with the suffering and losses of her people" during the war. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 5** Chang Lan, leader of Democratic League, announced dissolution of league. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 5** Government troops cleared north banks of Yangtze river in central China of communists. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 10** Fighting became intensified in outer perimeter of Shihchiachwang in Hopei. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 13** Program for Chinese-American agricultural cooperation was approved by Executive Yuan. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 14** The 15-day communist siege of Yulin in north Shensi was lifted. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 15** Legislative Yuan ratified Sino-American agreement providing relief for China. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 17** Chinese Government submitted formal proposals to U. S., Britain and Soviet Union for holding preliminary peace conference on Japan. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 21** First general election in China was held. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Nov. 26** U. S. Embassy spokesman in Nanking denied reports that Washington Congressional committee had suggested Tsingtao be granted to U. S. as a naval base. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 1** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs issued statement urging mutually satisfactory solution to question of China-Burma boundary demarcation through proper diplomatic channels in accordance with treaty provisions and international law. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 1** Yin Ju-ken, once head of a puppet "state" east of Peiping, was executed in Nanking. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 2** Executive Yuan approved immediate establishment of control offices in Shanghai, Tientsin, Canton and Hankow to prevent banks and business organizations from engaging in speculation and other illegal operations. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 3** All government forces in five northern provinces of Shansi, Hopei, Jehol, Suiyuan and Chahar were placed under unified command of Gen. Fu Tso-yi. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 5** Preliminary agreement was reached between Chinese Government and liaison representative of United Nations International Children's Emergency Fund, regarding half of US$3,500,000 allocated to China for buying cotton and dyes in U. S. — p. 127 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 7** George Yeh, vice-minister of foreign affairs, reiterated China's stand in note to Russia on Dec. 5 concerning composition and voting procedure of preliminary Japanese peace conference. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 8** China and U. S. reached agreement regarding transfer of U. S. vessels to China and training of Chinese naval personnel. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 10** Communist attempts to set up new base in Tapiehshan area were frustrated by government forces in central China. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 16** Wong Wen-hao, chairman of National Resources Commission, reported YVA plan was temporarily suspended, not abandoned, because of financial difficulties. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 22** Government recalled Gen. Feng Yu-hsiang, who was sent to U. S. to study water conservancy in 1946. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 25** National Assembly was postponed owing to delay of some local electoral offices in filing election returns and failure of others to hold election as scheduled. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 25** Government promulgated procedural rules for concluding Kuomintang political tutelage in mandate. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 26** Government announced decision to hold National Assembly on March 29 for election of China's new President and Vice-President. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 26** China exchanged notes with U. S., Britain, France, Belgium and the Netherlands, officially abolishing legation quarters in Peiping. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 29** Executive Yuan, in 16-point new ruling, restricted all bank loans and remittances, banned black market operations by all banking organizations and forbade acceptance by banks of gold and foreign currencies as securities or deposits. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1947 Dec. 29** Government garrison at Mukden fought with communists who had infiltrated into city's western suburbs. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 3** Finance Minister O. K. Yui reported to PPC Resident Committee that China's national budget amounted to CNC$9,300-billion. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 6** U. S. challenged USSR in Allied Control Council for Japan in Tokyo on whether Japanese army equipment captured in Manchuria by the Soviet Union had been turned over to Chinese Communists. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 7** Ministry of Foreign Affairs spokesman announced sending a Chinese technical mission to U. S. for consultation on China-aid program. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 8** Feng Yu-hsiang was expelled from Kuomintang. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 12** State Council made Peiping China's third auxiliary capital, the other two being Sian and Chungking. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 13** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh, lodged strong verbal protest to British Ambassador Sir Ralph Stevenson in Nanking against use of armed police force by Hongkong government in evicting Chinese residents in Kowloon city. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 14** Tsuyee Pei, former governor of Central Bank of China, left Shanghai as head of Chinese technical mission for Washington to discuss China-aid program. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 17** Anti-British demonstrations broke out in Canton and Shanghai. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 19** New unified command was created in northeast under Gen. Wei Li-huang. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 21** General election of members of Legislative Yuan, China's highest law-making body, was started. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 21** China formally asked British to compensate Chinese residents evicted from Kowloon city. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 26** Communist forces renewed attacks in Manchuria after two-week lull and captured Sinlitun, rail junction west of Mukden. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 28** Premier Chang Chun announced ten-point government program of "self-help," calling for financial and economic reform measures "to secure the maximum benefit from external aid." — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Jan. 29** Shanghai students, angered by arrests of demonstrators against British eviction of residents in Kowloon, clashed with police. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 3** British consulate in Mukden was closed. Six members of the closed American consulate left Mukden for Peiping. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 7** Liaoyang, south of Mukden, fell to communists after five days of fighting. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 9** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh, in report to State Council on Sino-British negotiations concerning Kowloon incident, reiterated China's determination to assert her sovereignty over Kowloon city. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 16** Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced that China and Australia had agreed to raise status of their respective diplomatic missions from legation to embassy. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 18** Ministry of Foreign Affairs spokesman said China would not recognize any government setup in Korea not in accordance with UN decision or Moscow agreement. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 18** President Truman sent message to Congress requesting US$570-million for aid to China until June 30, 1949, of which US$510-million would be utilized for food and relief and US$60-million for reconstruction. — p. 128 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 19** U. S. Ambassador J. Leighton Stuart in Nanking issued "personal message to the people of China" saying that U. S. aid alone would not be enough to bring political stability and economic recovery to China. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 20** Soviet Union announced it considered Russo-Chinese Non-Aggression Pact of 1937 automatically extended to 1950 since period for notice of abrogation had passed. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 21** U.P. report quoted Ambassador Stuart as saying that best possible solution of China's internal problems would be resumption of Government-Communist peace talks. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 21** Communists captured Anshan, south of Mukden. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 22** Ambassador Stuart, in interview with Central News, denied earlier statement to United Press. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 27** Communist forces besieged Yingkow. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 27** China and Italy concluded treaty of amity, replacing all pre-war treaties between the two countries. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 28** Government forces withdrew from Kaiyuan, northwest of Mukden. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Feb. 29** President Chiang assured Manchurian leaders of government's decision to hold northwest. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 1** Vice-President Sun Fo, at press conference in Taiwan, charged a U. S. information official with spreading reports of dissension among people of Taiwan against China. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 3** Treason charge was filed in Hopei High Court in Peiping against Henry Pu-yi, puppet emperor of "Manchukuo," now in Russian custody. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 4** Government reinforcements striking north from Great Wall reached point 30 miles west of Mukden to effect opening of western Liaoning corridor. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 7** Government forces breached siege of Szepingkai, between Chungchun and Mukden; communists claimed to have defeated government army in major battle at Ichuan, southeast of Yenan in Shensi province. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 10** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs lodged protest with Soviet Embassy in Nanking concerning shooting of air transport plane by two fighter planes bearing Soviet insignia over Gulf of Chili, about 120 miles north of Tientsin, on March 8. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 11** Communist troops broke into Loyang. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 11** Ministry of Foreign Affairs spokesman announced China's reply to Outer Mongolian Government's protest of "invasion" of latter's territory by Chinese troops. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 15** Government admitted loss of Kirin and Szepingkai in Manchuria. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 18** Government forces recaptured Loyang. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 24** Fuhsin and Tzechuan in Manchuria fell to communists. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 25** Sun Fo announced his candidacy for vice-president under the new constitution. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 27** President Chiang asked 300 Kuomintang members, who were elected to National Assembly as independents last November, to give up seats promised to minority parties. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 29** China's First National Assembly convened with 1,629 representatives attending opening session. In opening speech President Chiang called it "epoch-making event in the history of China." — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Mar. 31** Ministry of National Defense spokesman announced decision to abandon Lungkow, Penglai and Weihaiwei in easttern Shantung. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 4** Speaking before Kuomintang CEC Standing Committee, President Chiang declared he did not choose to run for presidency under constitution and would rather devote himself to unfinished task of suppressing Communist rebellion. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 7** Government military spokesman confirmed fall of Loyang, Honan. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 9** President Chiang in state-of-the-nation report to National Assembly admitted destruction of government's divisions in north China. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 14** Reviewing China's foreign relations before National Assembly, Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh accused U.S.S.R. of having violated 1945 agreements. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 15** 1,200 National Assembly delegates jointly proposed emergency powers for President during present crisis. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 16** President Chiang was nominated candidate for presidency by more than 2,000 delegates despite his earlier declaration that he would not seek the nation's highest post. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 18** The National Assembly approved by two-thirds majority vote four temporary provisions in constitution, granting emergency powers to President during the period of communist suppression campaign. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 19** Chiang Kai-shek was elected China's first President under new constitution by a vote of 2,430 out of 2,704 in the National Assembly. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 21** The National Assembly passed resolution asking U.S.S.R. to abide by 1945 Sino-Soviet treaty and return immediately to China all machinery and materials removed from Manchuria. — p. 129 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 22** Yenan fell to communists. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 24** Gen. Li Tsung-jen led five other vice-presidential candidates in first ballot in election of China's vice-president. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 27** Weihsien in Shantung fell to communists. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 28** Executive Yuan approved program submitted by provincial governments to terminate landlordism before 1949 in Kwangsi and Kansu. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 29** Gen. Li Tsung-jen was elected vice-president of China on fourth ballot. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Apr. 29** Government forces assisted by planes trapped some 50,000 communists around Hsienyang, west of Sian, they also captured Fengsiang and Paoki. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 1** President Chiang, officiating at the closing ceremony of National Assembly, called it "the greatest achievement of the Chinese people since the founding of the nation." — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 5** Premier Chang Chun and his cabinet resigned en bloc to give President Chiang a clean slate to reorganize executive branch of government. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 8** New Legislative Yuan session opened with two minority parties boycotting the meeting. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 13** Gen. Ku Chu-tung was appointed Chief of General Staff to succeed Gen. Chen Cheng. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 14** Gen. Hu Tsung-nan, government commander-in-chief in northwest, announced conclusion of 22-day battle on Shensi-Kansu border which resulted in a major defeat for the communists. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 17** Sun Fo and Chen Li-fu were elected president and vice-president, respectively, of Legislative Yuan. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 20** President Chiang Kai-shek and Vice-President Li Tsung-jen were inaugurated. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 24** Legislative Yuan approved appointment of Wong Wen-hao as premier of new constitutional government. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 25** New premier pledged to carry out radical and thorough reforms. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 31** President Chiang approved cabinet list submitted by Premier Wong Wen-hao. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 May 31** Government created Central China Communist Suppression Headquarters with Gen. Pai Chung-hsi as supreme commander. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 3** Francis Cardinal Spellman of New York arrived in Nanking for a two-day visit. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 4** U. S. Ambassador J. Leighton Stuart warned in statement that Chinese student agitation against U. S. policy in Japan "is seriously damaging the traditional cordiality between the United States and China." — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 5** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh declared that China was demanding safeguards against resurgence of Japanese militarism, although China supported the Potsdam Declaration and took cognizance of the necessity of enabling Japan gradually to become self-supporting. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 5** New Control Yuan session convened in Nanking. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 7** Roger D. Lapham, chief of China Mission of U. S. Economic Cooperation Administration, arrived in Shanghai. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))
