**1948 June 11** Premier Wong Wen-hao presented administrative program to the Legislative Yuan. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 22** President Chiang inspected Honan battle fronts as communists intensified attack on Kaifeng. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 24** Wang Chung-hui, elder statesman and world-famous jurist, and Chang Po-lin, noted educator, were appointed presidents of Judicial Yuan and Examination Yuan, respectively. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 25** Government troops recaptured Kaifeng, which fell to the communists two days ago. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 June 30** Premier Wong Wen-hao submitted budgetary bill to Legislative Yuan setting the figure of expenditure at one quadrillion Chinese dollars for second half of 1948. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 July 3** Economic Aid agreement between China and U. S. was signed in Nanking. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 July 5** Refugee students from northeast and police clashed in Peiping, resulting in over 20 casualties. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 July 6** Ministry of Industry and Commerce inaugurated. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 July 14** Twelve Grand Justices as nominated by the President were approved by the Control Yuan. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 July 22** President Chiang Kai-shek flew to Taiyuan to direct operations there. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 July 23** Budget submitted by Executive Yuan was approved by the Legislative Yuan with modifications. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 July 28** Premier Wong Wen-hao issued message to the nation, pointing out that Chinese Communists were one branch of international communism and appealing for solidarity to cope with the national crisis. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 5** Sino-American agreement for the establishment of a Joint Commission on Rural Reconstruction was signed in Nanking. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 5** Government troops recovered Siangyang, northwestern Hupeh. — p. 130 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 9** President and Madame Chiang Kai-shek left Nanking for Kuling. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 11** Sino-Italian barter agreement was signed. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 12** Foreign Minister Wang Shih-chieh announced recognition of the new Korean Government. Liu Yu-wan was appointed Chinese representative to Korea with ambassadorial rank. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 18** President Chiang returned to Nanking. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 19** By a Presidential mandate, the government adopted a new currency, the Gold Yuan, with the value of one Gold Yuan to three million units of fapi (legal tender). Measures for price control, control of gold and foreign exchange, and increase of the salary of government employees were also announced. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 20** Economic control supervisory commissioners were appointed for Shanghai, Tientsin and Canton areas. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 21** Ex-Premier Chang Chun left for Japan on an inspection tour. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 23** Gold Yuan notes were put into circulation. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Aug. 25** Hsia Wei and Chang Chen were appointed governors of Anhwei and Honan, respectively. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 1** Ting Chih-pan was appointed governor of Kiangsu. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 2** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced the Chinese Government had informed the Soviet Government that China was not prepared to extend the Sino-Soviet Aviation Pact. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 5** Central News reported from Moscow that Feng Yu-hsiang was killed in a fire on Soviet ship en route to Soviet Union. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 9** Committee for the Supervision of Constitutional Government was formed. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 10** Chang Chun met General MacArthur in Tokyo for the fourth time since his arrival in Japan. He returned to Shanghai on Sept. 13. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 13** Li Liang-yung was appointed governor of Fukien. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 15** President Chiang Kai-shek, in a radio broadcast, launched a nation-wide austerity movement. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 26** Government troops abandoned Tsinan, capital of Shantung. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Sept. 30** President Chiang flew to Peiping on an inspection tour. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 2** President Chiang flew to Mukden, and returned to Peiping the following day. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 5** President Chiang arrived in Tientsin, and inspected the Pohai ports on board a warship. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 8** President Chiang arrived in Shanghai from Peiping and returned to Nanking the following day. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 16** President Chiang arrived in Peiping again after a flying visit to Mukden. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 18** President Chiang flew to Mukden again and returned to Peiping after holding a military conference at the airfield. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 23** Changchun fell. Paotow, railway city in Suiyuan, and Chengchow, railway junction in Honan, evacuated by government troops. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 27** President Chiang paid flying visit to Chengteh, capital of Jehol. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 30** President Chiang returned to Nanking. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Oct. 31** President Chiang told U. S. correspondents that "if China's northeast could not be kept intact, Far Eastern peace would be impossible." — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 2** Evacuation of Mukden announced. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 4** Government forces evacuated Yingkow. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 9** Battle of Hsuchow began. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 10** Hsu Kan was appointed Finance Minister to succeed Wang Yun-wu, who resigned. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 13** Chen Pu-lei died. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 22** Madame Chiang Kai-shek, in a radio broadcast, appealed to the U. S. for aid. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 23** Paoting evacuated. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 25** Ambassador Wellington Koo called on President Truman, appealing for U. S. aid. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 26** The Legislative Yuan approved President Chiang's nomination of Sun Fo as president of the Executive Yuan to succeed Wong Wen-hao, who resigned. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 26** Chang Chun was appointed secretary-general of the Kuomintang Central Political Council. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 28** Madame Chiang Kai-shek left Shanghai for the United States. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Nov. 28** Chinwangtao evacuated. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 1** Madame Chiang arrived in Washington, D. C. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 3** Government troops evacuated Hsuchow. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 10** Madame Chiang met President Truman. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 23** New Cabinet under Sun Fo inaugurated. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 24** Tung Kwan-hsien and Liu Chien-chun elected president and vice-president, respectively, of the Legislative Yuan to succeed Sun Fo and Chen Li-fu, who became premier and minister without portfolio, respectively. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 25** Government troops evacuated Kalgan. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 29** Gen. Chen Cheng appointed governor of Taiwan. — p. 131 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1948 Dec. 30** Pao Erh Han (Burhan) appointed governor of Sinkiang. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 1** President Chiang expressed his wish for a peaceful settlement of the Government-Communist dispute. If the communists were sincere and a peaceful settlement could be realized, he said, he was not concerned about his own personal position. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 1** Premier Sun Fo, in a radio broadcast, expressed his willingness to strive for a peaceful settlement of the civil war. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 8** Deputy Premier and Foreign Minister Wu Te-chen received ambassadors of the United States, Great Britain, France and the Soviet Union, handing them identical copies of a memorandum to inquire their respective government's attitude toward the question of peaceful settlement of China's civil strife. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 13** Chairmen of provincial and municipal councils in north China appealed for cease fire in the Peiping-Tientsin area. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 14** Chinese communist party made an eight-point proposal for peace negotiations. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 14** Tientsin fell into communist hands. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 17** Delegation left Peiping for communist areas to negotiate local peace for Peiping. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 18** Generals Chang Chun, Chu Shao-liang, and Yu Han-mou were appointed pacification commissioners for Chungking, Fukien, and Canton, respectively. Tan En-po and Chen Cheng were made commanders-in-chief for the Nanking-Shanghai-Hangchow area and Taiwan, respectively. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 19** Executive Yuan proposed to the communists that both sides to cease fire first and then appoint delegates for peace negotiations. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 19** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs informed the various diplomatic missions in Nanking to prepare to move to Canton. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 19** Gen. Chu Shao-liang and Fan Tien were appointed governors of Fukien and Kiangsi, respectively. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 20** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced that China had received replies from the governments of the United States, Great Britain, France and the Soviet Union to the effect that it was difficult to mediate in the Government-Communist dispute. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 20** S. Y. Liu was appointed governor of the Central Bank of China. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 21** President Chiang Kai-shek announced his retirement from the presidency and left for Hangchow. Vice President Li Tsung-jen was to exercise presidential powers. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 21** The Executive Yuan received a reply from the communists insisting peace negotiations to be held first before a cease fire order was issued. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 22** The Executive Yuan announced the appointment of Shao Li-tze, Chang Chih-chung, and three others as peace negotiators with the communists. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 22** Local truce for the Peiping area was announced. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 22** General Hsueh Yueh was appointed governor of Kwangtung to succeed T. V. Soong. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 24** Acting President Li Tsung-jen ordered the abolition of martial law throughout the country, release of political prisoners, and the end of secret police activities. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 24** Kuomintang Central Political Council decided to continue to evacuate government organizations from Nanking. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 25** The Ministry of Foreign Affairs notified foreign diplomatic missions in Nanking to move to Canton. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 27** Acting President Li telegraphed Mao Tse-tung, informing Mao that the communist eight-point proposal might be used as a basis for peace negotiation and requesting him to appoint delegates for negotiation. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 28** Communist radio demanded the arrest of "war criminals" in the government and the arrest of Okamura, Japanese ex-commander-in-chief in China. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Jan. 28** Okamura was sent back to Japan. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Feb. 2** Sun Fo arrived in Canton. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Feb. 5** The Executive Yuan started functioning in Canton. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Feb. 9** Chang Fa-kwei was appointed commander-in-chief of Chinese Army. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Feb. 12** Tai Chuan-hsien, ex-president of the Examination Yuan, died in Canton. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Feb. 16** Chou Yen was appointed governor of Kiangsu. Chu Ting-ching was appointed governor of Hupeh. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Feb. 23** Chen Chi-tang was appointed governor of Hainan island. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Feb. 28** Premier Sun Fo returned to Nanking. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 8** Premier Sun Fo resigned. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 12** The Legislative Yuan approved the nomination of Gen. Ho Ying-chin as Premier. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 15** The Legislative Yuan adopted a resolution to simplify the government machinery. The Executive Yuan was to have eight ministries and two commissions. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 17** The Ministry of National Defense confirmed that the cruiser Chungking had defected to the communist side. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 20** Naval Headquarters announced the bombing of the Chungking by Chinese air force. — p. 132 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 24** Government peace delegation was reorganized; the five delegates were Shao Li-tze, Chang Chih-chung, Huang Shao-hsiung, Chang Shih-chao, and Li Chen. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 25** The Chinese Communist party headquarters was moved to Peiping. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 26** Communists announced that peace negotiations would be started in Peiping on April 1. Communist representatives were Chou En-lai, Lin Po-chu, Lin Piao, Yeh Chien-ying and Li Wei-han. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 26** Fighting continued in Anhwei province. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Mar. 31** Communists proposed all peace negotiations should be held in closed meetings, no news reporting was allowed. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 1** Government peace delegation flew to Peiping. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 1** Fu Tso-yi sent circular telegram to entire nation to express his political stand and to admit his past mistakes. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 6** Premier Ho Ying-chin arrived in Canton, returned to Nanking on April 8. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 14** Hu Lin, general manager of Ta Kung Pao, died in Shanghai. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 16** Fighting around Taiyuan was resumed. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 17** Communists demanded government acceptance of their eight-point proposal by April 20. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 19** Government rejected communist demand. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 20** British sloop Amethyst was fired on by communist artillery on the north shore of the Yangtze river. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 21** Communists resumed all-out offensive, and crossed the Yangtze river at Tikang, southwest of Nanking. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 22** Government issued order to continue resistance. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 22** Premier Ho Ying-chin was appointed concurrently Minister of National Defense in charge of the direction of all operations. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 23** Government forces evacuated Nanking. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 23** Communists occupied Kiangyin, important fort on the Yangtze west of Shanghai. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 23** Premier Ho arrived in Shanghai. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 23** Acting President Li Tsung-jen flew to Kweilin. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 24** Communist troops entered Nanking. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 24** Communist troops entered Taiyuan. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 27** President Chiang Kai-shek arrived in Shanghai, issued statement reiterating his determination to suppress the communist rebellion. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 27** Government recalled peace delegation from Peiping. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 27** Government troops evacuated Soochow. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 28** Government troops evacuated Ishing, Changhsing and Wushing. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 28** President Chiang left Shanghai for Amoy. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 29** The Executive Yuan announced all government departments had been moved to Canton. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Apr. 30** Hangchow was evacuated. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 4** Chinese Communists concluded mutual aid pacts with Korean and Burmese Communists. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 9** Acting President Li Tsung-jen arrived in Canton from Kweilin. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 12** Ministry of Foreign Affairs spokesman stated in Canton that China's foreign policy remained unchanged. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 12** Warships Tai Hu and Tai Chao, presented to China by the United States, arrived in Taiwan. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 13** Legislative Yuan adopted resolution to support the government to continue the war against the communists. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 15** Government troops evacuated Hankow and Wuchang. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 19** Ma Pu-fang was appointed director of military and political affairs for the northwest. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 20** Acting President Li Tsung-jen issued message to entire nation, expressing his determination to lead the nation in the anti-communist war. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 20** Government forces evacuated Sian. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 21** Kan Chieh-hou, acting President Li Tsung-jen's personal representative, arrived in San Francisco. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 27** Government forces evacuated Shanghai and Woosung. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 28** Shun Pao and Sin Wan Pao, largest daily newspapers in Shanghai, were closed by the communists. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 30** Premier Ho Ying-chin resigned. Acting President Li Tsung-jen nominated Chu Cheng as successor. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 31** The Legislative Yuan disapproved the nomination of Chu Cheng as premier. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 May 31** Soviet Ambassador Roschin left Canton for Russia via Paris. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 1** Sino-Soviet aviation pact was extended for five years. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 2** Acting President Li Tsung-jen nominated Yen Hsi-shan as premier to succeed Gen. Ho Ying-chin. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 3** Legislative Yuan approved the nomination of Yen Hsi-shan as premier. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 4** Premier Yen Hsi-shan announced that his new cabinet would be a war cabinet. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 4** Government forces evacuated Tsingtao. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 5** Premier Yen arrived in Canton from Taipeh. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 8** Martial law was declared in Hunan, Kiangsi, Chekiang, Fukien, Kwangtung and Kwangsi. — p. 133 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 12** George K. C. Yeh was appointed acting foreign minister, pending the return of Hu Shih from the United States. Hu Shih had been appointed foreign minister in Premier Yen Hsi-shan's new cabinet. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 15** Taiwan adopted a new currency. The new Taipi (New Taiwan Dollar) was linked with the United States currency at the rate of 5 to 1. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 15** The Shanghai Evening Post and Mercury, American-owned English-language paper in Shanghai, closed down. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 20** The Executive Yuan announced closure of communist-held sea ports, including Shanghai, Tientsin and Tsingtao, as of June 26. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 22** Kan Chieh-hou, Acting President Li's personal envoy, called on President Truman. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 June 24** Finance Minister Hsu Kan was appointed concurrently governor of the Central Bank of China. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 2** By a Presidential mandate, China adopted a new currency—the Silver Dollar Notes. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 4** President Chiang Kai-shek received two U. S. correspondents in Taipeh, asserting that as a revolutionary he was determined to fight the communists to the end. He appealed for American aid and pointed out that Chinese Communists were carrying out the policies of international communism. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 8** American vice consul Oliver in Shanghai was arrested by the communists, released the following day. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 10** President Chiang, in his capacity of Tsungtsai of the Kuomintang, flew to Baguio to meet President Quirino of the Philippines. The two presidents discussed the formation of a Pacific Union and a Far Eastern anti-communist alliance. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 12** President Chiang returned to Taiwan. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 13** Chinese legation in the Philippines was raised to the status of embassy. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 13** Shih Chueh was appointed garrison commander of the Chusan archipelago. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 14** President Chiang arrived in Canton. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 16** The Kuomintang Supreme Council was inaugurated under the presidency of Chiang Kai-shek. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 18** The standing committee of the Kuomintang adopted President Chiang's program for party reform. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 18** Gen. Chen Cheng was appointed director of military and political affairs for the southeast. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 21** President Chiang left Canton for Amoy. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 21** Government forces evacuated Nanchang. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 22** President Chiang arrived in Amoy. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 22** Communists closed down the U. S. Information Service Tientsin office. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 23** Coup d'etat was reported in Lhasa. Government personnel was ordered to leave Tibet. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 24** President Chiang returned to Taipeh. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 25** American-owned Chase Bank in Tientsin was closed down. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 26** Acting President Li Tsung-jen left Canton on an inspection tour of the southeast. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 27** Acting President Li arrived in Taipeh. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 27** Government forces evacuated Chuchow, railway junction in central Hunan. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 27** Ma Hung-kwei was appointed governor of Kansu. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 30** Acting President Li returned to Canton. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 30** Government forces evacuated Changteh, northwestern Hunan. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 31** British sloop Amethyst escaped from the Yangtze river. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 July 31** Russia concluded trade agreement with the so-called "Manchurian People's Democratic Authorities." — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 1** The Tsungtsai's Office of the Kuomintang was established in Taipeh. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 2** U. S. Ambassador J. Leighton Stuart arrived in Okinawa from Shanghai. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 3** President Chiang Kai-shek left Taipeh for Korea to meet President Syngman Rhee. He stopped at Tinghai for three days. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 4** Government ordered arrest of Cheng Chien, pacification commissioner for Hunan and Kiangsi, who defected to the communist side. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 5** The United States Government issued a White Paper, called "United States Relations with China." — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 5** Hunan Governor Chen Ming-jen defected to the Reds. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 5** Huang Chieh, vice minister of national defense, was appointed governor of Hunan. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 6** President Chiang arrived in Chinhai, South Korea, to confer with Korean President Rhee on the formation of a Pacific alliance. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 6** China protested to Russia against the latter's conclusion of a trade agreement with the so-called "Manchurian People's Democratic Authorities." — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 7** President Chiang returned to Taipeh. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 10** Ambassador J. Leighton Stuart arrived in Washington. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 12** Wu Te-chen, minister without portfolio, arrived in Tokyo to call on Gen. MacArthur. — p. 134 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 16** Chinese Ministry of Foreign Affairs issued statement on the United States White Paper. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 17** Government forces evacuated Foochow. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 20** Wu Te-chen returned to Taipeh. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 23** President Chiang Kai-shek flew to Canton to confer with Acting President Li Tsung-jen and Premier Yen Hsi-shan. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 24** President Chiang flew to Chungking. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 24** Government announced closure of sea ports north of the estuary of the Min river. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 25** Kwan Lin-chen was appointed commander-in-chief of the Chinese Army. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 27** Government forces evacuated Lanchow. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Aug. 29** Communists announced formation of the "Northeast People's Government." — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 2** Government ordered the arrest of Mao Tse-tung, Chu Teh and other communist leaders. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 4** Coup d'etat was reported to be taking place in Kunming, capital of Yunnan province. Governor Lu Han was reported to have declared independence. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 6** Governor Lu Han of Yunnan flew to Chungking to call on President Chiang Kai-shek. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 7** Premier Yen Hsi-shan flew to Chungking. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 8** Lu Han, governor of Yunnan, returned to Kunming. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 9** Premier Yen Hsi-shan returned to Canton. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 10** Government announced the dissolution of the Yunnan Provincial Council because of its anti-government activities. Leftist newspapers were ordered closed. National Yunnan University and other schools were dissolved, to be reorganized by the Ministry of Education. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 12** President Chiang Kai-shek flew to Chengtu. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 15** British Foreign Office announced recall of Ambassador Sir Ralph Stevenson from Nanking. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 16** Communists landed on Pingtan island off Fukien. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 17** President Chiang returned to Chungking. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 17** Korean consulate-general opened in Taipeh. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 21** Communist Leader Mao Tse-tung announced establishment of the "People's Republic of China" in Peiping. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 22** President Chiang returned to Canton via Kunming. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 22** Chinese chief delegate to UN asked nations to pay due attention to the China situation; accused USSR of undermining peace in the Far East. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 22** Ex-Premier Ho Ying-chin was appointed chairman of the Military Strategy Advisory Committee. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Sept. 27** China filed complaint in the United Nations General Assembly against the USSR, accusing the latter of aiding the Chinese Communists in the civil war and violating the Sino-Soviet Treaty of 1945 and the Charter of the United Nations. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 1** Communists announced establishment of the "People's Republic of China," with Mao Tse-tung as "Chairman" of the "People's Central Government," and Chou En-lai as "premier and concurrently foreign minister." — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 1** George K. C. Yeh was appointed Minister of Foreign Affairs. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 2** USSR announced recognition of the puppet Communist Government in Peiping. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 2** Chief of the General Staff Ku Chu-tung resigned. Hsiao Yi-shu was appointed Acting Chief of the General Staff. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 3** Foreign Minister George Yeh announced that China severed diplomatic relations with USSR. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 3** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek returned to Taipeh. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 3** Kwan Chi-yu was appointed Minister of Finance, succeeding Hsu Kan. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 4** Bulgaria, Rumania and Czechoslovakia recognized the Peiping regime. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 4** U. S. Department of State issued statement, reaffirming U. S. recognition of the National Government as the legal government of China. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 6** Government forces evacuated Chintang island, of the Chushan archipelago. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 6** Chinese Ambassador to France Tsien Tai resigned, M. L. Tuan was appointed Minister in charge of the Embassy. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 6** Nationalists scored victory at Chingshuping in central Hunan. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 6** Yugoslavia recognized the puppet Peiping government. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 7** Government troops evacuated Kukong. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 8** Government troops evacuated Hengyang. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 9** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek issued Double Tenth message, accusing Russian aggression on China and reiterating determination to fight communism to bitter end. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 10** Acting President Li Tsung-jen reiterated determination to continue the anti-communist war in an address observing the Double Tenth in Canton. — p. 135 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 10** Soviet Ambassador to the puppet government, Maj.-Gen. R. N. Roschin, arrived in Peiping. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 12** A presidential mandate announced the moving of the Central Government from Canton to Chungking. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 13** Acting President Li Tsung-jen left Canton for Kweilin. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 14** Government troops evacuated Canton. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 14** Acting President Li Tsung-jen arrived in Chungking. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 14** Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek returned to Taipeh after a three-day inspection tour of Tinghai. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 16** Kwangtung Governor Hsueh Yueh arrived in Hoikow, Hainan island. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 16** U. S. Senator Alexander Smith arrived in Taipeh; left the following day. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 17** Government troops evacuated Amoy. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 17** Central News Agency disclosed that Chinese Communists had concluded secret pacts with Russia in Harbin, giving Russia virtual control of China's political, economic and military affairs. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 17** Outer Mongolia recognized the puppet Peiping regime. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 17** U. S. Charge d'Affairs Robert Strong arrived in Chungking. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 20** Six U. S. Congressmen appealed for China aid and non-recognition of Chinese Communist regime. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 20** Communist cabinet was formed in Peiping. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 20** Government troops evacuated Taohua island of the Chushan group. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 21** Government troops evacuated Swatow. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 25** Taiwan celebrated fourth anniversary of Liberation Day. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 27** Communist invasion of Kingmen (Quemoy) island was repulsed. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Oct. 29** Angus Ward, U. S. consul-general at Mukden, and four of his staff members were imprisoned by the communists. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 10** Communist forces pushed to within 150 miles east of Chungking. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 11** Eleven planes of the Central Air Transport Corp. and the China National Aviation Corp. were flown by defectionists to Peiping from Hongkong. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 15** The S.S. Flying Cloud was shelled by Chinese naval vessel enforcing closure of Shanghai harbor. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 16** U. S. Secretary of State Dean Acheson declared in Washington that U. S. recognition of Chinese Communist regime was out of question as long as the communists continued to hold Angus Ward under arrest. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 16** U. S. protested to government for attack on the S.S. Flying Cloud. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 18** Communists sent radio message to the United Nations in an attempt to discredit the Chinese delegation. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 20** Acting President Li Tsung-jen arrived at Hongkong from Kweilin. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 22** Kweilin fell. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 25** T. F. Tsiang charged USSR with violating UN Charter and the Sino-Soviet Treaty of 1945 at the opening debate of the Political Committee of the UN General Assembly. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 25** Angus Ward and four of his staff members were convicted by the communists of beating a former Chinese employee. They were given sentences from three to six months imprisonment, which were commuted to deportation. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 25** U. S. Senator William F. Knowland and Maj.-Gen. Claire L. Chennault conferred with Generalissimo Chiang at Chungking. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 26** Dr. Tsiang introduced a four-point resolution at the UN Political Committee. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 27** Communists ordered deportation of all non-Chinese members of U. S. consulate at Mukden. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 28** U. S., together with Australia, Mexico, Pakistan and the Philippines, introduced a draft resolution on China-USSR dispute at UN. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 28** The S.S. Sir John Franklin was fired upon by Chinese war vessels enforcing closure of Shanghai harbor. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Nov. 30** Chungking fell to the communists. Generalissimo Chiang left Chungking for Chengtu. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 2** Regarding the S.S. Sir John Franklin incident, the U. S. requested China to stop any further attack on American vessels. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 4** Generalissimo Chiang announced plans to fly reinforcements from northwest China to defend the southwest. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 5** Cuba, Ecuador and Peru sponsored a joint resolution to refer China's case to the U.N. Interim Committtee (Little Assembly) for continued examination. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 6** Foreign Minister George Yeh, in a verbal reply to U. S. Charge d'Affairs at Hongkong Robert Strong, regarding the U. S. note of Dec. 2, reaffirmed China's right of closing the communist-held ports. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 7** Acting President Li Tsung-jen arrived in New York for medical treatment. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 8** The Cuba-Ecuador-Peru resolution, with subsequent amendments submitted by Lebanon and Uruguay, was adopted by the U.N. Political Committee which also accepted the U. S.-sponsored five-power resolution. — p. 136 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 8** Premier Yen Hsi-shan flew to Taipeh from Chengtu. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 8** Communist forces thrust into Yunnan. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 9** Cabinet started functioning in Taipeh. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 10** Generalissimo Chiang arrived at Taipei from Chengtu by plane. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 12** Angus Ward and party boarded the S. S. Lakeland Victory at Tientsin en route to U. S. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 16** Mao Tse-tung arrived in Moscow. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 16** Communist troops pushed to Chennankwan on Kwangsi-Indo-China border. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 17** Burma recognized Chinese Communist regime. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 17** U. S. State Department classified Shanghai as a "zone of danger" hazardous for American shipping. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 18** National Government severed diplomatic relations with Burma. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 18** Maj.-Gen. Claire L. Chennault and his partner Whiting Willauer bought Chinese Government's interests in the China National Aviation Corp. and the Central Air Transport Corp. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 21** K. C. Wu was appointed Governor of Taiwan to succeed General Chen Cheng. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 23** V. K. Wellington Koo, Chinese Ambassador at Washington, sent letter to U. S. State Department asking for American assistance to Taiwan. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 26** Communist forces entered Chengtu. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 28** China extended diplomatic recognition to Indonesia. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 28** Government planes blasted communist shiping along Luichow peninsula coast. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 29** Approaches to the Yangtze river and Shanghai within Chinese territorial waters declared mined. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))

**1949 Dec. 30** India recognized Chinese Communist regime. — p. 137 ([scan part 1, leaf 70](https://archive.org/details/china-handbook-1950/China%20Handbook%201950%20Part%201%20of%203/page/n70/mode/1up))
